Suppose we have a random Normal Normal sample: X_1,\ldots, X_n \stackrel{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(\mu, \gamma), where \mu is a known value and \gamma is unknown.

Derive the expected Fisher information 5.7 and the observed information 5.10 for \gamma using the whole random sample. Evaluate both at the MLE.

Solution

We compute \mathcal I_n(\gamma)=-\mathrm E_\gamma[\ell''(\gamma;\underline X)] and j_n(\gamma;\underline x)=-\ell''(\gamma;\underline x) separately.

Step 1: Compute log-likelihood

Since we have n i.i.d. \mathcal{N}(\mu, \gamma) with \mu known, the likelihood function is \begin{align*} L(\gamma;\underline{x}) &= \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\gamma}} \exp\left\{-\frac{(x_i - \mu)^2}{2\gamma}\right\} \\ &= (2 \pi)^{-n/2} \gamma^{-n/2} \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2\right\}. \end{align*} Therefore, the log-likelihood takes the form \ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) = -\frac{n}{2}\log(2\pi) - \frac{n}{2}\log(\gamma) - \frac{1}{2\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2.

Step 2: Compute Derivatives

We compute the first and second derivatives of the log-likelihood with respect to \gamma: \begin{align*} \frac{\partial}{\partial \gamma} \ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) &= -\frac{n}{2\gamma} + \frac{1}{2\gamma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2, \\ \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \gamma^2} \ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) &= \frac{n}{2\gamma^2} - \frac{1}{\gamma^3}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2. \end{align*} Step 3: Compute Fisher Information

We have \mathcal{I}(\gamma) = -\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{\partial^2}{\partial\gamma^2} \ell(\gamma; \underline{X})\right] = -\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{n}{2\gamma^2} - \frac{1}{\gamma^3}\sum_{i=1}^n (X_i - \mu)^2\right], where the random component of the expectation is the random data X_i\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu, \gamma). Therefore, manipulating the expectation: \begin{align*} \mathcal{I}(\gamma) &= -\frac{n}{2\gamma^2} + \frac{1}{\gamma^3}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{E}[(X_i - \mu)^2] \\ &= -\frac{n}{2\gamma^2} + \frac{1}{\gamma^3}\sum_{i=1}^n \gamma, \end{align*} using the fact that each X_i - \mu \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \gamma) and so \mathrm{E}[(X_i - \mu)^2] = \mathrm{Var}(X_i) = \gamma. Continuing: \begin{align*} \mathcal{I}(\gamma) &= -\frac{n}{2\gamma^2} + \frac{n\gamma}{\gamma^3} \\ &= \frac{n}{2\gamma^2} \end{align*}

For the realised data, the observed information is j_n(\gamma;\underline x) =-\frac{n}{2\gamma^2} +\frac{1}{\gamma^3}\sum_{i=1}^n(x_i-\mu)^2.

Step 4: Compute MLE

The interior MLE solves: \begin{align*} \frac{\partial}{\partial \gamma} \ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) &= 0 \\ -\frac{n}{2\gamma} + \frac{1}{2\gamma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2 &= 0 \\ \Rightarrow \hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}} &= \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2 \end{align*}

Step 5: Evaluate the information at the MLE

The plug-in expected information is \begin{align*} \mathcal{I}(\hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}}) &= \frac{n}{2\hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}}^2} \\ &= \frac{n^3}{2\left(\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2\right)^2}. \end{align*} Because \sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2=n\hat\gamma_{\mathrm{MLE}}, evaluating the observed information also gives j_n(\hat\gamma_{\mathrm{MLE}};\underline x) =-\frac{n}{2\hat\gamma_{\mathrm{MLE}}^2} +\frac{n\hat\gamma_{\mathrm{MLE}}}{\hat\gamma_{\mathrm{MLE}}^3} =\frac{n}{2\hat\gamma_{\mathrm{MLE}}^2}.

Important 30: Question 50: Asymptotic Posterior for the Variance Parameter

Suppose we have an independent and identically distributed sample \underline{x} = (x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n)^\top from a distribution with PDF f(x \mid \gamma) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi \gamma}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{x^2}{2\gamma}\right], \qquad \gamma > 0.

Determine the asymptotic posterior distribution for \gamma.

Solution

Step 1: Likelihood

f(\underline{x} \mid \gamma) = \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi \gamma}} \exp\!\left[-\frac{x_i^2}{2\gamma}\right] = (2\pi \gamma)^{-n/2} \exp\!\left[-\frac{1}{2\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2\right].

Step 2: Log-likelihood

\ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) = -\frac{n}{2}\log(2\pi) - \frac{n}{2}\log(\gamma) - \frac{1}{2\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2.

Step 3: Derivatives

\frac{\partial \ell}{\partial \gamma} = -\frac{n}{2\gamma} + \frac{1}{2\gamma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2,

\frac{\partial^2 \ell}{\partial \gamma^2} = \frac{n}{2\gamma^2} - \frac{1}{\gamma^3}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2.

Let
a = \sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2.

Step 4: MLE

Solving \partial \ell / \partial \gamma = 0:

-\frac{n}{2\gamma} + \frac{a}{2\gamma^2} = 0 \quad\Rightarrow\quad \hat{\gamma} = \frac{a}{n} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2.

Step 5: Fisher Information

For one observation:

\ell(\gamma) = -\tfrac12\log(2\pi\gamma) - \tfrac{x^2}{2\gamma}, and so \frac{\partial^2 \ell}{\partial \gamma^2} = \frac{1}{2\gamma^2} - \frac{x^2}{\gamma^3}.

Taking expectation under X \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\gamma):

\mathcal{I}(\gamma) = -\mathrm{E}\!\left[\frac{\partial^2 \ell}{\partial \gamma^2}\right] = -\left(\frac{1}{2\gamma^2} - \frac{\gamma}{\gamma^3}\right) = \frac{1}{2\gamma^2}.

Thus for n observations:

\mathcal{I}_n(\gamma) = \frac{n}{2\gamma^2}.

Evaluated at the MLE:

\mathcal{I}_n(\hat{\gamma}) = \frac{n}{2\hat{\gamma}^2}.

Step 6: Asymptotic posterior

By Theorem 6.2

\gamma \mid \underline{x} \approx \mathcal{N}\!\left( \hat{\gamma},\; I_n(\hat{\gamma})^{-1} \right) = \mathcal{N}\!\left( \hat{\gamma},\; \frac{2\hat{\gamma}^2}{n} \right).

That is,

\boxed{ \gamma \mid \underline{x} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\!\left( \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2,\; \frac{2}{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2\right)^2 \right). }

Important 31: Question 51: Asymptotic Posterior for Poisson Data

Suppose we have independent Poisson Poisson observations X_1,\ldots, X_n \mid \lambda \sim \mathrm{Pois}(\lambda)

  1. Determine the general asymptotic posterior distribution 6.2 for \lambda.
  2. Suppose we observe n = 100 data points with sample mean \bar{x} = 3.21. Compute the asymptotic posterior distribution.
Solution
  1. General Asymptotic Posterior.
    1. Log-Likelihood: Since each observation is independent X_i \mid \lambda \sim \mathrm{Pois}(\lambda), we have \begin{align*} f(\underline{x} \mid \lambda) &= \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{\lambda^{x_i} e^{-\lambda}}{x_i!} \\ &= \lambda^{n\bar{x}} e^{-n\lambda} \left(\prod_{i=1}^n x_i! \right)^{-1}. \end{align*} Therefore, the log-likelihood is \log f(\underline{x} \mid \lambda) = n\bar{x} \log(\lambda) - n\lambda - \sum_{i=1}^n \log(x_i!).
    2. Derivatives: The derivatives of the log-likelihood are \begin{align*} \frac{\partial}{\partial \lambda} \log f(\underline{x} \mid \lambda) &= \frac{n\bar{x}}{\lambda} - n \\ \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \lambda^2} \log f(\underline{x} \mid \lambda) &= -\frac{n\bar{x}}{\lambda^2}. \end{align*}
    3. MLE: Solving \frac{n\bar{x}}{\lambda} - n = 0 yields the maximum likelihood estimator \hat{\lambda} = \bar{X}.
    4. Fisher Information: The Fisher information 5.7 for the whole sample is thus \begin{align*} \mathcal{I}_n(\lambda) &= -\mathrm{E}\left[-\frac{n\bar{X}}{\lambda^2}\right] \\ &= \frac{n}{n\lambda^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\mathrm{E}\left[X_i\right] \\ &= \frac{n}{\lambda}, \end{align*} since each \mathrm{E}[X_i] = \lambda.
    5. Plug in the MLE: The expected Fisher information evaluated at the MLE is \mathcal{I}_n(\hat{\lambda}) = \frac{n}{\bar{x}}. (For the Poisson model, the observed information evaluated at the MLE happens to have the same value.)
    6. Asymptotic Posterior Distribution: The asymptotic posterior distribution 6.2 is thus \lambda \mid \underline{x} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\left(\bar{x}, \frac{\bar{x}}{n}\right)
  2. Plug in data. Using the derived result from the first part and the provided summaries of the data n = 100 and \bar{x} = 3.21, we have \lambda \mid \underline{x} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\left(3.21, 0.0321\right)
Important 32: Question 52: Asymptotic Posterior for Exponential Data

Suppose we have independent Exponential observations with rate parameter \lambda: X_1,\ldots,X_n \mid \lambda \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\lambda).

  1. Determine the general asymptotic posterior distribution 6.2 for \lambda.
  2. Suppose we observe n = 200 data points with sample mean \bar{x} = 7.14. Compute the asymptotic posterior distribution.
Solution

  1. General Asymptotic Posterior

    1. Likelihood and log-likelihood

    The density of each X_i is f(x_i \mid \lambda) = \lambda e^{-\lambda x_i}.

    Thus the likelihood is f(\underline{x} \mid \lambda) = \prod_{i=1}^n \lambda e^{-\lambda x_i} = \lambda^n \exp\!\left(-\lambda\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\right).

    The log-likelihood is \ell(\lambda; \underline{x}) = n\log\lambda - \lambda\sum_{i=1}^n x_i = n\log\lambda - n\lambda \bar{x}.

    1. Derivatives

    \frac{\partial}{\partial \lambda} \ell(\lambda; \underline{x}) = \frac{n}{\lambda} - n\bar{x}, \qquad \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \lambda^2} \ell(\lambda; \underline{x}) = -\frac{n}{\lambda^2}.

    1. MLE

    Solve \frac{n}{\lambda} - n\bar{x} = 0, which gives the maximum likelihood estimator \hat{\lambda} = \frac{1}{\bar{X}}.

    1. Fisher information

    The Fisher information 5.7 for the whole sample is thus \mathcal{I}_n(\lambda) = -\mathrm{E}\left[-\frac{n}{\lambda^2}\right] = \frac{n}{\lambda^2}.

    Evaluating the expected Fisher information at the MLE gives \mathcal{I}_n(\hat{\lambda}) = \frac{n}{\hat{\lambda}^2} = n\bar{x}^2. Here the observed information is j_n(\lambda;\underline x)=n/\lambda^2, so it agrees with expected information for every \lambda.

    1. Asymptotic posterior distribution

    By Theorem 6.2 \lambda \mid \underline{x} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\!\left( \hat{\lambda},\; \mathcal{I}_n(\hat{\lambda})^{-1} \right) = \mathcal{N}\!\left( \frac{1}{\bar{x}},\; \frac{1}{n\bar{x}^{2}} \right).

  2. Plugging in data

    For n = 200 and \bar{x} = 7.14, \hat{\lambda} = \frac{1}{ 7.14} \approx 0.14, and the variance is \frac{1}{n\bar{x}^2} = \frac{1}{200 \cdot 7.14^2} \approx 9.807 \times 10^{-5}.

    Thus the asymptotic posterior is \lambda \mid \underline{x} \approx \mathcal{N}\!\left(0.14,\; 9.807 \times 10^{-5}\right).

ImportantQuestion 53: Bayesian Inference for the Click-through Rate on Instagram

Software engineers at Meta are interested in the click-through rate \theta of a new method of displaying advertisements on Instagram. The click-through rate is the proportion of clicks out of the total number of impressions of an online advertisement.

They test the new method on users of their website, obtaining a total of 20 impressions. From prior experience, they specify \theta \sim \mathrm{Beta}(1,200).

  1. Let X \mid \theta denote the number of clicks out of the total number of impressions. What is an appropriate model for X \mid \theta?
  2. After running the test, the posterior obtained was \theta \mid x \sim \mathrm{Beta}(1,220). Determine the number of successful clicks x.
  3. Construct a 90\% highest density interval (HDI) for \theta.

Hint: The following plot may be helpful.

Show code 
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import beta

set_plot_theme("light")

theta_grid = np.linspace(0, 0.03, 100)
beta_pdf = beta.pdf(theta_grid, 1, 220)

plt.figure(figsize=(5,3))
plt.plot(theta_grid, beta_pdf)
plt.xlabel(r"$\theta$")
plt.ylabel("Density")
plt.title(r"PDF of $\theta \mid x \sim \mathrm{Beta}(1,220)$")
plt.xlim(0, 0.03)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()  

set_plot_theme("dark")

plt.figure(figsize=(5,3))
plt.plot(theta_grid, beta_pdf)
plt.xlabel(r"$\theta$")
plt.ylabel("Density")
plt.title(r"PDF of $\theta \mid x \sim \mathrm{Beta}(1,220)$")
plt.xlim(0, 0.03)
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()
Solution

1. Likelihood model

An appropriate model is the Binomial: X \mid \theta \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n=20,\ \theta).

2. Recovering x from the posterior

We have \begin{align*} \pi(\theta|x) &\propto \theta^x(1-\theta)^{20-x} \times \theta^0 (1-\theta)^{199} \\ &\propto \theta^{x + 1 -1}(1-\theta)^{220-x - 1}. \end{align*} The posterior being \theta \mid x \sim \textrm{Beta}(1, 220) implies x = 0.

3. A 90\% highest density interval for \theta

From the provided plot, a 90\% highest density interval would be of the form 0 < \theta < c, where c is the solution of the equation \int_0^c \pi(\theta|x)\,\mathrm{d}\theta = 0.9. Note that \frac{\Gamma(220 + 1)}{\Gamma(1) \Gamma(220)} = \frac{220!}{219!} = 220. Therefore, \begin{align*} \int_0^c \pi(\theta|x)\,\mathrm{d}\theta &= \int_0^c 220(1-\theta)^{219}\,\mathrm{d}\theta \\ &= \left[-(1-\theta)^{220}\right]_{0}^c \\ &= 1-(1-c)^{220}. \end{align*} Therefore, we seek c such that \begin{align*} 1-(1-c)^{220} &= 0.9 \\ (1-c)^{220} &= 0.1 \\ c &= 1 - 0.1^{1/220} = 0.01041 \text{ (4 s.f.)}. \end{align*} So, the 90\% highest density interval is \theta \in (0, 0.01041).

Important 33: Question 54: Approximate MLE-based Confidence Interval for Geometric Data

Suppose we have n i.i.d. geometric observations X_1,\dots,X_n \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathrm{Geometric}(p).

  1. Using the asymptotic distribution of the MLE, construct a general 100(1-\alpha)\% approximate CI for p.
  2. Suppose we observe a random sample of size n = 100 from a geometric distribution, and the sample mean is \bar{x} = 9.84. Using the asymptotic normal approximation of the MLE, compute an approximate 95\% confidence interval for the parameter p. You may use z_{0.975}=1.960.
Solution
  1. Approximate CI. Note that, in order to compute the approximate CI we require both the MLE of p and the Fisher information.
    • Step 1: Compute Log Likelihood For i.i.d. geometric data, the likelihood is \begin{align*} L(p; \underline{x}) &= \prod_{i=1}^n p(1-p)^{x_i - 1} \\ &= p^n(1-p)^{n(\bar{x} - 1)}. \end{align*} The log-likelihood is thus \ell(p; \underline{x}) = n \log p + n(\bar{x} - 1) \log(1-p).
    • Step 2: Compute Derivatives of the Log-Likelihood The derivatives of the log-likelihood are \begin{align*} \frac{\partial}{\partial p} \ell(p; \underline{x}) &= \frac{n}{p} - \frac{n(\bar{x} - 1)}{1 - p}, \\ \frac{\partial^2}{\partial p^2} \ell(p; \underline{x}) &= -\frac{n}{p^2} - \frac{n(\bar{x} - 1)}{(1 - p)^2}. \end{align*}
    • Step 3: Compute MLE We compute the MLE by solving \begin{align*} \frac{n}{p} - \frac{n(\bar{x} - 1)}{1 - p} &= 0 \\ n(1-p) - n(\bar{x} - 1)p &= 0 \\ n\bar{x} p &= n \\ \Rightarrow \hat{p}_{\mathrm{MLE}} &= \frac{1}{\bar{x}}. \end{align*}
    • Step 4: Compute Fisher Information The Fisher information for the whole sample is: \begin{align*} \mathcal{I}_n(p) &= -\mathrm{E}\left[-\frac{n}{p^2} - \frac{n(\bar{X} - 1)}{(1 - p)^2}\right] \\ &= \frac{n}{p^2} - \frac{n}{(1-p)^2} + \frac{n}{(1-p)^2}\mathrm{E}[\bar{X}] \\ &= \frac{n}{p^2} - \frac{n}{(1-p)^2} + \frac{n}{p(1-p)^2}, \end{align*} since \mathrm{E}[X_i] = 1/p and so \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{E}[X_i] = 1/p. Simplifying further, we obtain \mathcal{I}_n(p) = n\frac{(1-p)^2 - p^2 + p}{p^2(1-p)^2} = \frac{n}{p^2(1-p)}. Substituting p = \hat{p}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = 1/\bar{x} into the expected Fisher information gives \mathcal{I}_n(\hat{p}_{\mathrm{MLE}}) = \frac{n \bar{x}^2}{(1-1/\bar{x})} = \frac{n\bar{x}^3}{\bar{x} - 1}. For this model, the realised observed information evaluated at the MLE has the same value, but the two definitions are conceptually distinct.
    • Step 5: Compute Approximate CI For an interior true parameter 0<p<1 and large n, the MLE has approximate sampling distribution \hat{p}_{\mathrm{MLE}} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\bigl(p, \mathcal{I}_n(p)^{-1}\bigr). Evaluating the information at the observed MLE gives the estimated standard error. When \bar{x}>1, the resulting approximate 100(1-\alpha)\% confidence interval is \frac{1}{\bar{x}} \pm z_{1-\alpha/2} \sqrt{\frac{\bar{x} - 1}{n \bar{x}^3}}.
  2. Compute the Approximate CI using data. Using the derived formula from the first part \frac{1}{\bar{x}} \pm z_{1-\alpha/2} \sqrt{\frac{\bar{x} - 1}{n \bar{x}^3}}. we plug in the given values \bar{x} = 9.84 and n=100. The relevant quantile is z_{0.975} = 1.96. Therefore, we obtain 0.1016 \pm 1.96 \times 0.0096 \approx 0.1016 \pm 0.0189. Therefore, an approximate 95\% confidence interval for p is \boxed{ (0.083, 0.121), \quad \text{(to three decimal places)}. }
Important 34: Question 55: Asymptotic Distribution of the Exponential MLE

Suppose X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n is an i.i.d. random sample from an \mathrm{Exp}(\lambda) distribution, with density f(x \mid \lambda) = \lambda e^{-\lambda x}, \qquad x \ge 0.

The maximum likelihood estimator for \lambda is known to be \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = \frac{1}{\bar{X}}. Compute the approximate sampling distribution 5.9 of \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MLE}}.

Solution

Step 1: Recall the general asymptotic MLE result

From the asymptotic normality of the MLE, for regular models: \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MLE}} \;\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\; \mathcal{N}\!\left( \lambda,\; \mathcal{I}_n(\lambda)^{-1} \right), \quad \text{for large } n, where \mathcal{I}_n(\lambda) is the Fisher information for the whole sample.

Step 2: Compute the Fisher information

The log-likelihood is \ell(\lambda; \underline{x}) = n\log \lambda - n\lambda \bar{x}.

Differentiate: \frac{\partial}{\partial \lambda} \ell(\lambda; \underline{x}) = \frac{n}{\lambda} - n\bar{x}, \qquad \frac{\partial^2 }{\partial \lambda^2} \ell(\lambda; \underline{x}) = -\frac{n}{\lambda^2}.

So the Fisher information for the whole sample is \mathcal{I}_n(\lambda) = -\mathrm{E}\left[-\frac{n}{\lambda^2}\right] = \frac{n}{\lambda^2}.

Thus \mathcal{I}_n(\lambda)^{-1} = \frac{\lambda^2}{n}.

Step 3: Asymptotic distribution of the MLE

Plugging this into the general asymptotic MLE result: \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MLE}} \;\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\; \mathcal{N}\!\left( \lambda,\; \frac{\lambda^2}{n} \right), \quad \text{as } n \to \infty.

In practice, since \lambda is unknown, we use the plug-in \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = 1/\bar{x} into the Fisher information, resulting in: \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MLE}} \;\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\; \mathcal{N} \left( \lambda,\; \frac{1}{n\bar{x}^2} \right),

Important 35: Question 56: Normal Data: Asymptotic Distribution of the MLE of the variance

Suppose X_1,\ldots, X_n \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(\mu, \gamma), with mean \mu known.

The maximum likelihood estimator for \gamma is known to be \hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2. Compute the approximate sampling distribution 5.9 of \hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}}.

Solution

Step 1: Recall the general asymptotic MLE result

From the asymptotic normality of the MLE, for regular models: \hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}} \;\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\; \mathcal{N}\!\left( \gamma,\; \mathcal{I}_n(\gamma)^{-1} \right), \quad \text{for large } n, where \mathcal{I}_n(\gamma) is the Fisher information for the whole sample.

Step 2: Compute the Fisher information

The log-likelihood is \ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) = -\frac{n}{2} \log(2\pi\gamma) - \frac{1}{2\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2

Differentiate: \frac{\partial}{\partial \gamma} \ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) = -\frac{n}{2\gamma} + \frac{1}{2\gamma^2} \sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2 \qquad \frac{\partial^2 }{\partial \gamma^2} \ell(\gamma; \underline{x}) = \frac{n}{2\gamma^2} - \frac{1}{\gamma^3} \sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2.

So the Fisher information for the whole sample is \begin{align*} \mathcal{I}_n(\gamma) &= -\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{n}{2\gamma^2} - \frac{1}{\gamma^3} \sum_{i=1}^n (X_i - \mu)^2\right] \\ &= -\frac{n}{2\gamma^2} + \frac{1}{\gamma^3}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{E}[(X_i - \mu)^2] \\ &= \frac{n}{2\gamma^2}, \end{align*} since \mathrm{E}[(X_i - \mu)^2] = \mathrm{Var}(X_i) = \gamma.

Thus \mathcal{I}_n(\lambda)^{-1} = \frac{2\gamma^2}{n}.

Step 3: Asymptotic distribution of the MLE

Plugging this into the general asymptotic MLE result: \hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}} \;\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\; \mathcal{N}\!\left( \gamma,\; \frac{2\gamma^2}{n} \right).

In practice, since \gamma is unknown, we use the plug-in \hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2 into the Fisher information, resulting in: \hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}} \;\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\; \mathcal{N} \left( \gamma,\; \frac{2(\hat{\gamma}_{\mathrm{MLE}})^2}{n} \right) = \mathcal{N} \left( \gamma,\; \frac{2\left(\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2\right)^2}{n^3} \right),

Important 36: Question 57: Large-Sample Approximate Confidence Interval for a Proportion

A university careers office surveys a random sample of n = 200 graduates to estimate the proportion who found full-time employment within 3 months of graduation. Out of the 200 respondents, 146 report being in full-time employment.

Assuming a Binomial model Binomial X \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n = 200 , \, p), where p is the true employment rate, use the Central Limit Theorem 4.1 to construct an approximate 95% confidence interval for p.

Solution

Step 1: Model and estimator

The sample proportion is \hat{p} = \frac{X}{n} = \frac{146}{200} = 0.73.

Step 2: Approximate distribution (CLT)

For large n, \hat{p} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\!\left(p,\, \frac{p(1-p)}{n}\right). Replacing p by \hat{p} gives the estimated standard error \mathrm{SE}(\hat{p}) = \sqrt{\frac{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{0.73(0.27)}{200}} \approx 0.0317.

Step 3: Construct 95% confidence interval

Using the Normal quantile z_{0.975} = 1.96, \hat{p} \pm z_{0.975}\,\mathrm{SE}(\hat{p}) = 0.73 \pm 1.96(0.0317) = (0.668,\, 0.792).

Step 4: Interpretation

We are approximately 95% confident that the true proportion of graduates in full-time employment within 3 months lies between 66.8% and 79.2%.

Important 37: Question 58: Large-Sample Hypothesis Test for a Proportion

A public health agency claims that at least 70% of adults in a city receive an annual flu vaccination. To assess this claim, a random sample of n = 180 adults is surveyed. Of these, 117 report having received a flu vaccination in the past year.

Assuming a Binomial model Binomial X \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n = 180,\, p), where p is the true proportion of adults who receive an annual flu vaccination, use the Central Limit Theorem 4.1 to perform a 5% significance level hypothesis test of the agency’s claim.

Solution

The sample proportion is \hat{p} = \frac{X}{n} = \frac{146}{200} = 0.73,

and, for large n, the CLT gives us \hat{p} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\!\left(p,\, \frac{p(1-p)}{n}\right).

Step 1: State the hypotheses

The claim “at least 70%” corresponds to H_0: p = 0.70, \qquad H_1: p < 0.70. This is a one-sided (left-tailed) test.

Step 2: Compute the test statistic

The sample proportion is \hat{p} = \frac{117}{180} = 0.65.

Under the null hypothesis, the standard error is \mathrm{SE}_0 = \sqrt{\frac{p_0(1-p_0)}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{0.7(0.3)}{180}} \approx 0.0342.

The observed test statistic is T_{\mathrm{obs}}= \frac{\hat{p} - p_0}{\mathrm{SE}_0} = \frac{0.65 - 0.70}{0.0342} \approx -1.46.

Step 3: Determine the rejection region / p-value

At the 5% significance level for a one-sided test, the critical value is z_{0.05} = -1.645.

Since -1.46 > -1.645, the test statistic does not fall in the rejection region.

Equivalently, the p-value is \mathrm{Pr}(Z \le -1.46) \approx 0.072, which is greater than 0.05.

Step 4: Conclusion

There is insufficient evidence at the 5% significance level to reject the agency’s claim. The data do not provide strong enough evidence to conclude that fewer than 70% of adults receive an annual flu vaccination.

Important 38: Question 59: Large-Sample Hypothesis Test for a Poisson Rate

A city council claims that the average number of pothole reports received per day is at least 12. To assess this claim, the number of pothole reports is recorded for a random sample of n = 50 days. Across these 50 days, a total of X = 540 pothole reports are logged.

Assuming a Poisson model Poisson X_i \stackrel{\text{iid}}{\sim} \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda), \qquad i=1,\dots,n, where \lambda is the true mean number of pothole reports per day, use the Central Limit Theorem 4.1 to perform a 5% significance level hypothesis test of the council’s claim.

Solution

Let X_1,\dots,X_n be the daily counts. The observed sample mean is \bar{x} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i = \frac{540}{50} = 10.8.

For a Poisson random variable, \mathrm{E}[X_i]=\lambda and \mathrm{Var}(X_i)=\lambda. For large n, the CLT gives \bar{X} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N} \left(\lambda, \frac{\lambda}{n}\right).

Step 1: State the hypotheses

The claim “at least 12 per day” corresponds to H_0: \lambda = 12, \qquad H_1: \lambda < 12. This is a one-sided (left-tailed) test.

Step 2: Compute the test statistic

Under the null, the standard error is \mathrm{SE}_0 = \sqrt{\frac{\lambda_0}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{12}{50}} \approx 0.490.

The observed test statistic is T_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \lambda_0}{\mathrm{SE}_0} = \frac{10.8 - 12}{0.490} \approx -2.45.

Step 3: Determine the rejection region / p-value

At the 5% significance level for a one-sided test, the critical value is z_{0.05} = -1.645.

Since -2.45 < -1.645, the test statistic falls in the rejection region.

Equivalently, the p-value is \mathrm{Pr}(Z \le -2.45) \approx 0.007.

Step 4: Conclusion

There is sufficient evidence at the 5% significance level to reject the council’s claim. The data provide evidence that the true mean number of pothole reports per day is less than 12.

Important 39: Question 60: Large-Sample Two-Sided Hypothesis Test for a Poisson Rate

A call centre manager claims that the average number of customer complaint calls received per hour is 5. To assess this claim, the number of complaint calls is recorded over a random sample of n = 60 one-hour periods. Across these 60 hours, a total of X = 360 complaint calls are received.

Assuming a Poisson model X_i \stackrel{\text{iid}}{\sim} \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda), \qquad i=1,\dots,n, where \lambda is the true mean number of complaint calls per hour, use the Central Limit Theorem to perform a 1% significance level two-sided hypothesis test of the manager’s claim.

You may use z_{0.995}=2.576.

Solution

Let X_1,\dots,X_n be the hourly counts. The sample mean is \bar{X} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i = \frac{360}{60} = 6.

For a Poisson random variable, \mathrm{E}[X_i]=\lambda and \mathrm{Var}(X_i)=\lambda. For large n, the CLT gives \bar{X} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\left(\lambda, \frac{\lambda}{n}\right).

Step 1: State the hypotheses

The claim “\lambda = 5 per hour” corresponds to H_0: \lambda = 5, \qquad H_1: \lambda \neq 5. This is a two-sided test.

Step 2: Compute the test statistic

Under the null, the standard error is \mathrm{SE}_0 = \sqrt{\frac{\lambda_0}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{5}{60}} \approx 0.289.

The observed test statistic is T_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \lambda_0}{\mathrm{SE}_0} = \frac{6 - 5}{0.289} \approx 3.46.

Step 3: Determine the rejection region / p-value

At the 1% significance level for a two-sided test, the critical values are \pm z_{0.995} = \pm 2.576.

Since |3.46| > 2.576, the test statistic falls in the rejection region.

Equivalently, the p-value is 2\,\mathrm{Pr}(Z \ge 3.46) \approx 2(0.00027) \approx 0.00054.

Step 4: Conclusion

There is sufficient evidence at the 1% significance level to reject the manager’s claim. The data provide evidence that the true mean number of complaint calls per hour is different from 5.

Important 40: Question 61: Large-Sample Two-Sided Hypothesis Test for an Exponential Rate

A hospital administrator claims that the average waiting time for a non-urgent blood test is 20 minutes. To assess this, a random sample of n = 64 waiting times is collected. The sample mean waiting time is \bar{X} = 24.0 minutes.

Assuming an Exponential model Exponential X_i \stackrel{\text{iid}}{\sim} \mathrm{Exponential}(\lambda), \qquad i=1,\dots,n, where \lambda is the rate parameter (so that \mathrm{E}[X_i]=1/\lambda), use the Central Limit Theorem 4.1 to perform a 5% significance level two-sided hypothesis test of the administrator’s claim.

Solution

For an exponential random variable with rate \lambda, \mathrm{E}[X_i] = \frac{1}{\lambda}, \qquad \mathrm{Var}(X_i) = \frac{1}{\lambda^2}.

For large n, the CLT gives \bar{X} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}, \frac{1}{n\lambda^2}\right).

Step 1: State the hypotheses

The claim “mean waiting time is 20 minutes” is H_0: \frac{1}{\lambda} = 20 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad H_0: \lambda = \lambda_0 = \frac{1}{20} = 0.05, with the two-sided alternative H_1: \lambda \neq 0.05.

Step 2: Compute the test statistic

Under the null hypothesis, the standard error of \bar{X} is \mathrm{SE}_0 = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n\lambda_0^2}} = \frac{1}{\lambda_0\sqrt{n}} = \frac{1}{0.05\sqrt{64}} = \frac{1}{0.05\cdot 8} = 2.5.

The observed test statistic is T_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - 1/\lambda_0}{\mathrm{SE}_0} = \frac{24 - 20}{2.5} = 1.60.

Step 3: Determine the rejection region / p-value

At the 5% significance level for a two-sided test, the critical values are \pm z_{0.975} = \pm 1.96.

Since |1.60| < 1.96, the test statistic does not fall in the rejection region.

Equivalently, the p-value is approximately 2\,\mathrm{Pr}(Z \ge 1.60) \approx 2(0.0548) \approx 0.110.

Step 4: Conclusion

There is insufficient evidence at the 5% significance level to reject the administrator’s claim. The data do not provide strong evidence that the mean waiting time differs from 20 minutes.

Important 41: Question 62: One-Sample Z-test and t-test for a Normal Mean

A materials scientist is studying the tensile strength (in MPa) of a newly developed polymer. From a random sample of n = 25 specimens, the observed sample mean strength is \bar{X} = 52.4 \text{ MPa}.

It is reasonable to assume that tensile strength measurements are normally distributed and independent: X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2), where \mu is the true mean tensile strength.

The manufacturer claims that the mean tensile strength of the polymer is \mu_0 = 50 \text{ MPa}.

You are asked to assess this claim at the 5% significance level under two different assumptions.

(a) Assume that the population variance is known to be \sigma^2 = 16 MPa^2.

(b) Assume instead that the population variance is unknown, and the sample standard deviation is s = 4.5 \text{ MPa}.

In each case, perform an appropriate two-sided hypothesis test of H_0 : \mu = 50, \qquad H_1 : \mu \neq 50.

Solution

(a) Z-test (known variance)

Since \sigma^2 is known and the data are normally distributed, a one-sample Z-test is appropriate.

The standard error of the sample mean is \mathrm{SE} = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{25}} = 0.8.

The observed Z-statistic is Z_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\mathrm{SE}} = \frac{52.4 - 50}{0.8} = 3.00.

At the 5% significance level for a two-sided test, the critical values are \pm z_{0.975} = \pm 1.96.

Since |3.00| > 1.96, we reject H_0.

Equivalently, the p-value is 2\,\mathrm{Pr}(Z \ge 3.00) \approx 0.0027, which is less than 0.05.

Conclusion (Z-test): There is strong evidence that the mean tensile strength differs from 50 MPa.

(b) t-test (unknown variance)

When \sigma^2 is unknown and estimated from the data, we use a one-sample t-test.

The estimated standard error is \widehat{\mathrm{SE}} = \frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{4.5}{\sqrt{25}} = 0.9.

The observed t-statistic is T_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}} = \frac{52.4 - 50}{0.9} \approx 2.67.

Under H_0, this statistic follows a t-distribution with n - 1 = 24 degrees of freedom.

At the 5% significance level for a two-sided test, the critical values are \pm t_{0.975,,24} \approx \pm 2.064.

Since |2.67| > 2.064, we reject H_0.

The p-value is approximately 2\,\mathrm{Pr}(T_{24} \ge 2.67) \approx 0.013, which is less than 0.05.

Conclusion (t-test): There is sufficient evidence to conclude that the mean tensile strength differs from 50 MPa.

Final remark

Both tests lead to rejection of H_0, but the t-test is more conservative, reflecting the additional uncertainty introduced by estimating the variance from the data.

Important 42: Question 63: VO_2 Max: One-Sided One-Sample Z-Test and t-Test for a Normal Mean

A sports physiologist is evaluating whether a new training programme increases athletes’ VO_2 max (in mL/kg/min). From a random sample of n = 41 athletes who completed the programme, the sample mean VO_2 max is \bar{X} = 47.2 \text{ mL/kg/min}.

It is reasonable to assume the VO_2 max measurements are normally distributed and independent across athletes: X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2), where \mu is the true mean VO_2 max after completing the programme.

Previous seasons’ data suggest the historical mean is \mu_0 = 45 \text{ mL/kg/min}.

You are asked to test whether the programme increases mean VO_2 max, using a one-sided test at significance level \alpha = 0.01, under two different assumptions.

(a) Assume the population variance is known to be \sigma^2 = 36 (so \sigma = 6).

(b) Assume instead the population variance is unknown, and the sample standard deviation is s = 6.4.

In each case, perform the appropriate hypothesis test of H_0 : \mu = 45, \qquad H_1 : \mu > 45.

Solution

(a) Z-test (known variance)

With known \sigma, use the one-sample Z-test.

The standard error is \mathrm{SE} = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{6}{\sqrt{41}} \approx 0.937.

The observed Z-statistic is Z_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\mathrm{SE}} = \frac{47.2 - 45}{0.937} \approx 2.35.

At significance level \alpha = 0.01 for a right-tailed test, the critical value is z_{0.99} \approx 2.326.

Since 2.35 > 2.326, we reject H_0.

Equivalently, the p-value is \mathrm{Pr}(Z \ge 2.35) \approx 0.0094, which is less than 0.01.

Conclusion (Z-test): There is sufficient evidence at the 1% level to conclude that the programme increases mean VO_2 max.

(b) t-test (unknown variance)

With unknown \sigma, use the one-sample t-test.

The estimated standard error is \widehat{\mathrm{SE}} = \frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{6.4}{\sqrt{41}} \approx 1.00.

The observed t-statistic is T_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}} = \frac{47.2 - 45}{1.00} \approx 2.20.

Under H_0, this statistic follows a t-distribution with n-1 = 40 degrees of freedom.

At \alpha = 0.01 (right-tailed), the critical value is t_{40,,0.99} \approx 2.423.

Since 2.20 < 2.423, we do not reject H_0.

The p-value is approximately \mathrm{Pr}(T_{40} \ge 2.20) \approx 0.017, which is greater than 0.01.

Conclusion (t-test): There is insufficient evidence at the 1% level to conclude that the programme increases mean VO_2 max.

Final remark

The Z-test rejects while the t-test does not at the 1% level. This illustrates how accounting for uncertainty in the variance (via the t-test use of the sample variance) leads to a more conservative inference.

Important 43: Question 64: Sodium Content: One-Sided One-Sample Z-Test and t-Test for a Normal Mean

A food manufacturer monitors the average sodium content (in mg) of a packaged soup product. Regulations require that the mean sodium content should not exceed 820 mg per serving. To check compliance, a random sample of n = 40 servings is tested, yielding a sample mean of \bar{X} = 790 \text{ mg}.

It is reasonable to model sodium content as independent normal observations X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2), where \mu is the true mean sodium content per serving.

You are asked to test whether the product’s mean sodium content is below the regulatory limit, using a one-sided test at significance level \alpha = 0.01, under two different assumptions.

(a) Assume the population variance is known to be \sigma^2 = 10{,}000 mg^2 (so \sigma = 100 mg).

(b) Assume instead that the population variance is unknown, and the sample standard deviation is s = 110 \text{ mg}.

In each case, perform the appropriate hypothesis test of H_0 : \mu = 820, \qquad H_1 : \mu < 820.

Solution

(a) Z-test (known variance)

With known \sigma, use the one-sample Z-test.

The standard error is \mathrm{SE} = \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{100}{\sqrt{40}} \approx 15.81.

The observed Z-statistic is Z_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\mathrm{SE}} = \frac{790 - 820}{15.81} \approx -1.90.

At significance level \alpha = 0.01 for a left-tailed test, the critical value is z_{0.01} \approx -2.326.

Since -1.90 > -2.326, we do not reject H_0.

Equivalently, the p-value is \mathrm{Pr}(Z \le -1.90) \approx 0.029, which is greater than 0.01.

Conclusion (Z-test): There is insufficient evidence at the 1% level to conclude that the mean sodium content is below 820 mg.

(b) t-test (unknown variance)

With unknown \sigma, use the one-sample t-test.

The estimated standard error is \widehat{\mathrm{SE}} = \frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{110}{\sqrt{40}} \approx 17.39.

The observed t-statistic is T_{\mathrm{obs}} = \frac{\bar{X} - \mu_0}{\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}} = \frac{790 - 820}{17.39} \approx -1.73.

Under H_0, this statistic follows a t-distribution with n-1 = 39 degrees of freedom.

At \alpha = 0.01 (left-tailed), the critical value is t_{39,\,0.01} \approx -2.426.

Since -1.73 > -2.426, we do not reject H_0.

The p-value is approximately \mathrm{Pr}(T_{39} \le -1.73) \approx 0.046, which is greater than 0.01.

Conclusion (t-test): There is insufficient evidence at the 1% significance level to conclude that the product’s mean sodium content is below the regulatory limit.

Final remark

Both tests lead to the same conclusion. The t-test is slightly more conservative due to the additional uncertainty in estimating the variance.

Important 44: Question 65: MAP Estimation in a Defect-Rate Problem

A factory produces electronic components, each of which may be defective with some unknown probability p.
To assess the defect rate, engineers inspect a random sample of n = 40 components and find that
x = 7
are defective.

To incorporate previous experience, the quality-control team places a prior distribution
p \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a,b), \qquad a = 2,\; b = 8.

You may assume the data follow the model X \mid p \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n,p).

  1. Derive the posterior distribution \pi(p \mid x).
  2. Compute the Maximum a Posteriori (MAP) estimator \hat{p}_{\mathrm{MAP}}.
  3. Interpret your MAP estimate in the context of the defect-rate problem.
Solution

  1. Posterior Distribution

    The likelihood is f(x \mid p) = \binom{n}{x} p^{\,x} (1-p)^{\,n-x}.

    The prior is \pi(p) = \frac{\Gamma(a+b)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)} p^{\,a-1}(1-p)^{\,b-1}.

    Therefore the posterior (dropping constants not depending on p) is \pi(p \mid x) \propto p^{\,x+a-1} (1-p)^{\,n-x+b-1}.

    This is recognised as a Beta distribution: p \mid x \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a + x,\; b + n - x) = \mathrm{Beta}(2 + 7,\; 8 + 40 - 7) = \mathrm{Beta}(9,\; 41).

  2. MAP Estimator

    For a \mathrm{Beta}(\alpha,\beta) distribution with \alpha>1 and \beta>1, the mode is provided in closed form, yielding the MAP: \hat{p}_{\mathrm{MAP}} = \frac{\alpha - 1}{\alpha + \beta - 2}.

    Here \alpha = 9 and \beta = 41, so \hat{p}_{\mathrm{MAP}} = \frac{9 - 1}{9 + 41 - 2} = \frac{8}{48} = \frac{1}{6} \approx 0.1667.

    Alternatively, we can derive this using the approach outlined here 6.4:

    We have \pi(p \mid x) \propto p^{\,x+a-1} (1-p)^{\,n-x+b-1}. Taking logs: \log \pi(p \mid x) = (x + a - 1)\log p + (n - x + b - 1)\log(1 - p) + C, for some p-independent constant C.

    Differentiating with respect to p: \frac{\partial}{\partial p} \log \pi(p \mid x) = \frac{x + a - 1}{p} - \frac{n - x + b - 1}{1 - p}.

    Setting this equal to 0 and solving for p: \begin{align*} \frac{x + a - 1}{p} - \frac{n - x + b - 1}{1 - p} &= 0 \\[4pt] \frac{x + a - 1}{p} &= \frac{n - x + b - 1}{1 - p} \\[4pt] (x + a - 1)(1 - p) &= (n - x + b - 1)p \\[4pt] x + a - 1 &= (n - x + b - 1)p + (x + a - 1)p \\[4pt] x + a - 1 &= (n + a + b - 2)p \\[4pt] \Rightarrow\quad \hat{p}_{\text{MAP}} &= \frac{x + a - 1}{n + a + b - 2}. \end{align*}

    Plugging in n = 40,\; x = 7,\; a = 2,\; b = 8 gives \hat{p}_{\text{MAP}} = \frac{7 + 2 - 1}{40 + 2 + 8 - 2} = \frac{8}{48} = \frac{1}{6} \approx 0.1667.

  3. Interpretation

    Based on both prior information and the observed data, the MAP estimator suggests that the defect probability is approximately \hat{p}_{\mathrm{MAP}} \approx 0.17.

    This is slightly lower than the raw sample proportion
    \frac{x}{n} = \frac{7}{40} = 0.175, reflecting the influence of the prior belief that defects are relatively uncommon.

Important 45: Question 66: MAP Estimation for a Call-Arrival Rate

A customer support centre receives phone calls according to a Poisson process with unknown rate \lambda (calls per hour).

To model the long-run arrival rate, analysts place a prior distribution \lambda \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a,b), \qquad a = 3,\; b = 1.5, where we use the shape–rate parameterisation: \lambda \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a,b) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \pi(\lambda) \propto \lambda^{a-1} e^{-b\lambda}.

Over a monitoring period of T = 10 hours, they observe a total of y = 47 calls.

Assume that, conditional on \lambda, the total number of calls in T hours follows Y \mid \lambda \sim \mathrm{Poisson}(T\lambda).

  1. Derive the posterior distribution \pi(\lambda \mid y).
  2. Compute the Maximum a Posteriori (MAP) estimator \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MAP}}.
  3. Interpret your MAP estimate in the context of the call-arrival rate (give its units and practical meaning).
Solution

  1. Posterior Distribution

    The likelihood for the total number of calls Y in T hours, given \lambda, is f(y \mid \lambda) = \Pr(Y = y \mid \lambda) = \frac{(T\lambda)^y e^{-T\lambda}}{y!}.

    The prior is \pi(\lambda) = \frac{b^a}{\Gamma(a)} \lambda^{a-1} e^{-b\lambda}, \qquad a = 3,\; b = 1.5, using the shape–rate parameterisation.

    Therefore, the posterior density (dropping constants not depending on \lambda) is \pi(\lambda \mid y) \propto \lambda^{\,y} e^{-T\lambda} \cdot \lambda^{\,a-1} e^{-b\lambda} = \lambda^{\,y+a-1} e^{-(T+b)\lambda}.

    This is recognised as a Gamma distribution with updated shape and rate: \lambda \mid y \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a + y,\; b + T) = \mathrm{Gamma}(3 + 47,\; 1.5 + 10) = \mathrm{Gamma}(50,\; 11.5).

  2. MAP Estimator

    For a \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha,\beta) distribution (shape–rate) with \alpha > 1, the mode is \lambda_{\text{mode}} = \frac{\alpha - 1}{\beta}, which is also the MAP estimator in this conjugate setting: \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MAP}} = \frac{\alpha - 1}{\beta}.

    Here \alpha = 50 and \beta = 11.5, so \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MAP}} = \frac{50 - 1}{11.5} = \frac{49}{11.5} \approx 4.26 \;\text{calls per hour}.

    Alternatively, we can derive this by maximising the log-posterior directly (as in the general MAP procedure 6.4):

    From above, \pi(\lambda \mid y) \propto \lambda^{\,y+a-1} e^{-(T+b)\lambda}. Taking logs, \log \pi(\lambda \mid y) = (y + a - 1)\log \lambda - (T + b)\lambda + C, for some \lambda-independent constant C.

    Differentiating with respect to \lambda: \frac{\partial}{\partial \lambda} \log \pi(\lambda \mid y) = \frac{y + a - 1}{\lambda} - (T + b).

    Setting this equal to 0 and solving: \begin{align*} \frac{y + a - 1}{\lambda} - (T + b) &= 0 \\[4pt] \frac{y + a - 1}{\lambda} &= T + b \\[4pt] \lambda &= \frac{y + a - 1}{T + b}. \end{align*}

    Substituting y = 47,\; a = 3,\; T = 10,\; b = 1.5 gives \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MAP}} = \frac{47 + 3 - 1}{10 + 1.5} = \frac{49}{11.5} \approx 4.26, consistent with the mode formula above.

  3. Interpretation

    The MAP estimate suggests that the underlying call-arrival rate is approximately \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MAP}} \approx 4.26 \;\text{calls per hour}.

    For comparison, the MLE based only on the data is \hat{\lambda}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = \frac{y}{T} = \frac{47}{10} = 4.7 \;\text{calls per hour}.

    The MAP estimate is slightly lower than the MLE, reflecting the influence of the prior belief
    (encoded by the \mathrm{Gamma}(3,1.5) prior) that the call rate is somewhat smaller than what the current data alone would suggest.

Important 46: Question 67: Unbiased Estimation and Efficiency in the Normal Model

For n\ge 2, suppose that X_1, \ldots, X_n are independent \mathcal{N}(\mu, \theta) random variables, where \mu is a known constant and \theta is the unknown variance. Let \bar{X} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i.

  1. Let \hat{\theta} = \frac{1}{c}\sum_{i=1}^n (X_i - \bar{X})^2 be an estimator of \theta.

    1. Determine the distribution of \bar{X} and hence show that
      \mathrm{E}[\bar{X}] = \mu and \mathrm{E}[\bar{X}^2] = \theta/n + \mu^2.

    2. Show that \mathrm{E}[X_i^2] = \theta + \mu^2 for any i = 1, \ldots, n.

    3. Using the fact that \mathrm{E}[X_i\bar{X}] = \theta/n + \mu^2 for any i, or by another method,
      derive the value of c that ensures \hat{\theta} is unbiased.

  2. Show that for any unbiased estimator \hat{\theta} of \theta, it holds that \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}] \geq \frac{2\theta^2}{n}.

  3. Let \hat{\theta}' = \frac{1}{n+2}\sum_{i=1}^n (X_i - \mu)^2 be another estimator of \theta.

    Determine whether \hat{\theta}' has a lower mean-squared error (MSE) than any unbiased estimator of \theta.

    Hint: If X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \theta), then \mathrm{Var}[(X-\mu)^2] = 2\theta^2.

Solution

(1a)
Since \bar{X} is the sample mean of i.i.d. normals, it follows that
\bar{X} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \theta/n).
Hence: \mathrm{E}[\bar{X}] = \mu, \qquad \mathrm{E}[\bar{X}^2] = \mathrm{Var}[\bar{X}] + \mathrm{E}[\bar{X}]^2 = \frac{\theta}{n} + \mu^2.

(1b)
For any i, \mathrm{E}[X_i^2] = \mathrm{Var}[X_i] + \mathrm{E}[X_i]^2 = \theta + \mu^2.

(1c)
We have: \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] = \frac{1}{c}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{E}[(X_i - \bar{X})^2] = \frac{1}{c}\sum_{i=1}^n \Big(\mathrm{E}[X_i^2] - 2\mathrm{E}[X_i\bar{X}] + \mathrm{E}[\bar{X}^2]\Big).

Substituting known expectations: \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] = \frac{1}{c}\sum_{i=1}^n \big(\theta+\mu^2 - 2(\theta/n+\mu^2) + \theta/n + \mu^2\big) = \frac{n-1}{c}\theta.

Thus, \hat{\theta} is unbiased if and only if c = n-1.

(2)

The likelihood is: L(\theta) = (2\pi\theta)^{-n/2} \exp\!\left[-\frac{1}{2\theta}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i-\mu)^2\right].

Hence the log-likelihood: \ell(\theta) = -\frac{n}{2}\log(2\pi) - \frac{n}{2}\log(\theta) - \frac{1}{2\theta}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i-\mu)^2.

Differentiating twice: \frac{\partial^2 \ell}{\partial \theta^2} = \frac{n}{2\theta^2} - \frac{1}{\theta^3}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2.

The Fisher information is: \mathcal{I}(\theta) = -\mathrm{E}\!\left[\frac{\partial^2 \ell}{\partial \theta^2}\right] = \frac{n}{2\theta^2}.

By the Cramér–Rao inequality: \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}] \ge \frac{1}{\mathcal{I}(\theta)} = \frac{2\theta^2}{n}.

(3)

Compute the bias: \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}'] = \frac{1}{n+2}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{E}[(X_i - \mu)^2] = \frac{n\theta}{n+2}, \qquad \text{Bias}(\hat{\theta}') = -\frac{2\theta}{n+2}.

Variance: \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}'] = \frac{1}{(n+2)^2}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{Var}[(X_i - \mu)^2] = \frac{2n\theta^2}{(n+2)^2}.

Hence: \mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\theta}') = \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}'] + \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta}')^2 = \frac{2n\theta^2}{(n+2)^2} + \frac{4\theta^2}{(n+2)^2} = \frac{2\theta^2}{n+2}.

Since \frac{2\theta^2}{n+2} < \frac{2\theta^2}{n} for all n > 0,
\hat{\theta}' has a smaller mean-squared error than any unbiased estimator of \theta.

Important 47: Question 68: Likelihood Ratio Test for Extreme Weather Events

The number of extreme weather events observed over a year is denoted by x,
modelled as a Poisson random variable X \sim \mathrm{Poisson}(\theta).

We wish to test the hypothesis H_0: \theta = 10,
where \theta = 10 represents “standard weather conditions.”

  1. Show that the maximum likelihood estimate for \theta is \hat{\theta} = x.
    (You may assume that any local optimum is a global maximum.)

  2. Suppose x = 15 extreme weather events are observed. Show that \max_{\theta \in \Theta} L(\theta; x) = \frac{15^{15} e^{-15}}{15!}, \qquad L(\theta = 10; x) = \frac{10^{15} e^{-10}}{15!}, where L(\theta; x) is the likelihood for \theta given x = 15.

  3. Show that the likelihood ratio test statistic is \lambda(x) = e^5 \left(\tfrac{2}{3}\right)^{15}, and compute T_{\mathrm{obs}} = -2\log \lambda(x) to 3 significant figures.

  4. Is there sufficient evidence to reject H_0 at the 10\% significance level?

Solution

1. Maximum likelihood estimate

Since we only have a single \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda) observation, the likelihood is: L(\theta; x) = \frac{\theta^x e^{-\theta}}{x!}. The log-likelihood: \ell(\theta; x) = x \log \theta - \theta - \log(x!). Differentiating: \frac{\partial }{\partial \theta} \ell(\theta; x) = \frac{x}{\theta} - 1 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \hat{\theta} = x. Hence, the MLE is \hat{\theta} = x.

2. Evaluate the likelihoods

Given x = 15: \max_{\theta \in \Theta} L(\theta; x) = L(15; x) = \frac{15^{15} e^{-15}}{15!}, \qquad L(\theta = 10) = \frac{10^{15} e^{-10}}{15!}.

3. Likelihood ratio statistic

\lambda(x) = \frac{L(10; x)}{L(15; x)} = \frac{10^{15} e^{-10}}{15^{15} e^{-15}} = e^5 \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{15}. Hence: T_{\mathrm{obs}} = -2 \log \lambda = -2[15 \log(2/3) + 5] = 2.16 \quad (\text{3 s.f.}).

4. Hypothesis decision

By Wilk’s theorem, under H_0, the test statistic is approximately T \sim \chi^2_1.

The 10% upper-tail critical value is 2.71.

Since T_{\mathrm{obs}} = 2.16 < 2.71, there is insufficient evidence to reject H_0 at the 10% level.

Therefore, we do not reject the hypothesis of standard weather conditions.

ImportantQuestion 69: Bias, Variance, and Mean Squared Error

  1. Let \hat{\theta} be an estimator of a parameter \theta. Provide definitions for the following:

    • The bias of \hat{\theta}.
    • The variance of \hat{\theta}.
    • The mean squared error (MSE) of \hat{\theta}.

  2. Is an unbiased estimator always preferred to a biased estimator? Justify your answer.

  3. Prove that the mean squared error of \hat{\theta} is equal to its variance plus its squared bias: \mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\theta}) = \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}) + \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta})^2.

Solution

(1)

  • \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta}) = \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] - \theta
  • \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}] = \mathrm{E}[(\hat{\theta} - \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}])^2]
  • \mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\theta}) = \mathrm{E}[(\hat{\theta} - \theta)^2]

(2)

An unbiased estimator is not always preferred.
If a biased estimator has a smaller MSE than an unbiased one, the biased estimator may be preferable because MSE accounts for both variance and bias.

(3)

Let \bar{\theta} = \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}]. Then: \begin{align*} \mathrm{E}[(\hat{\theta} - \theta)^2] &= \mathrm{E}[(\hat{\theta} - \bar{\theta} + \bar{\theta} - \theta)^2] \\ &= \mathrm{E}[(\hat{\theta} - \bar{\theta})^2] + 2(\bar{\theta} - \theta)\mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta} - \bar{\theta}] + (\bar{\theta} - \theta)^2. \end{align*}

Since \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta} - \bar{\theta}] = 0, this simplifies to: \mathrm{E}[(\hat{\theta} - \theta)^2] = \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}] + (\bar{\theta} - \theta)^2 = \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}] + \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta})^2.

Thus, \boxed{\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\theta}) = \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}) + \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta})^2.}

Important 48: Question 70: Likelihood Ratio Test for the Lifespan of a Magical Artefact

The time (in years) until a magical artefact loses its power is modelled as an exponential random variable X \sim \mathrm{Exponential}(1/\theta), where \theta > 0 is the mean lifespan.

According to archived lore, an authentic artifact should have mean lifespan \theta_0 = 50 years.

We wish to test the hypothesis
H_0 : \theta = 50
against the alternative that the true lifespan differs.

  1. Show that the maximum likelihood estimate of \theta is \hat{\theta} = x, where x is the observed lifespan.
    (You may assume that any critical point is a global maximum.)

  2. Suppose a recovered artifact is observed to last x = 60 years. Show that \max_{\theta \in \Theta} L(\theta; x) = \frac{1}{60} e^{-1}, \qquad L(\theta = 50; x) = \frac{1}{50} e^{-60/50}, where L(\theta; x) denotes the likelihood under mean \theta.

  3. Show that the likelihood ratio statistic is \lambda(x) = \frac{5}{6} \, e^{-1/5}, and compute the observed statistic T_{\mathrm{obs}} = -2 \log \lambda(x) to 3 significant figures.

  4. Based on the asymptotic \chi^2_1 distribution of T_{\mathrm{obs}}, is there sufficient evidence to reject H_0 at the 10\% significance level?

Solution

1. Maximum likelihood estimate

Exponential density: f(x \mid \theta) = \frac{1}{\theta} e^{-x/\theta}, \qquad x \ge 0. Likelihood for a single observation: L(\theta; x) = \frac{1}{\theta} e^{-x/\theta}. Log-likelihood: \ell(\theta; x) = -\log \theta - \frac{x}{\theta}. Differentiate: \frac{\partial \ell}{\partial \theta} = -\frac{1}{\theta} + \frac{x}{\theta^2} = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \hat{\theta} = x.

2. Evaluate the likelihoods

At the MLE \theta = 60: L(60; x) = \frac{1}{60} e^{-60/60} = \frac{1}{60} e^{-1}. At \theta = 50: L(50; x) = \frac{1}{50} e^{-60/50} = \frac{1}{50} e^{-6/5}.

3. Likelihood ratio statistic

\lambda(x) = \frac{L(50; x)}{L(60; x)} = \frac{\tfrac{1}{50} e^{-6/5}} {\tfrac{1}{60} e^{-1}} = \frac{60}{50} \, e^{-6/5 + 1} = \frac{6}{5} e^{-1/5}.

Thus: \lambda(x) = \frac{5}{6} e^{-1/5}.

Then: T_{\mathrm{obs}} = -2 \log \lambda(x) = -2\left[\log(5/6) - \frac{1}{5}\right] = 0.514 \quad (\text{3 s.f.}).

4. Hypothesis decision

Under H_0, T \sim \chi^2_1 approximately.

The 10\% upper-tail critical value is 2.71.

Since: T_{\mathrm{obs}} = 0.514 < 2.71, we do not reject H_0 at the 10\% significance level.

There is insufficient evidence that the artifact’s lifespan differs from 50 years.

Important 49: Question 71: Properties of the Maximum Likelihood Estimator

Let \underline{X} = (X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n) be a random sample from a continuous random variable X whose probability density function is given by f(x \mid \theta) = \begin{cases} \big(\theta(1 + x)^{\frac{1+\theta}{\theta}} \big)^{-1}, & x \ge 0, \\ 0, & \text{otherwise}, \end{cases} where 0 < \theta < 1.

Answer the following:

  1. Show that the log-likelihood can be written as
    \ell(\theta \mid \underline{x}) = -n \log \theta - \frac{1+\theta}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^n \log(1 + x_i).

  2. Show that the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) of \theta is
    \hat{\theta} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \log(1 + x_i).

  3. Using the fact that \mathrm{E}[\log(1 + X)] = \theta, show that \hat{\theta} is an unbiased estimator for \theta.

  4. Is \hat{\theta} also an asymptotically unbiased estimator for \theta?

Solution

(a) Derivation of the log-likelihood

The likelihood function is
L(\theta; \underline{x}) = \prod_{i=1}^n f(x_i \mid \theta) = \theta^{-n} \prod_{i=1}^n (1 + x_i)^{-\frac{1+\theta}{\theta}}. Taking logs gives
\ell(\theta \mid \underline{x}) = -n \log \theta - \frac{1+\theta}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^n \log(1 + x_i).

(b) Finding the MLE

Differentiate with respect to \theta:
\frac{\partial \ell}{\partial \theta} = -\frac{n}{\theta} + \frac{1}{\theta^2}\sum_{i=1}^n \log(1 + x_i). Setting this equal to zero:
-\frac{n}{\hat{\theta}} + \frac{1}{\hat{\theta}^2}\sum_{i=1}^n \log(1 + x_i) = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \hat{\theta} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \log(1 + x_i).

(c) Unbiasedness

Given \mathrm{E}[\log(1 + X)] = \theta,
\mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{E}[\log(1 + X_i)] = \frac{1}{n} \cdot n\theta = \theta. Hence, \hat{\theta} is unbiased.

(d) Asymptotic unbiasedness

Since \hat{\theta} is unbiased for every finite n,
\lim_{n \to \infty} \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] = \theta. Therefore, \hat{\theta} is also asymptotically unbiased.

Important 50: Question 72: Estimation from a Uniform Distribution

Suppose that X_1, \ldots, X_n are independent random variables with X_i \sim U(\theta, 1), where \theta is an unknown parameter of interest.

Consider the estimator \hat{\theta} = \min(X_1, \ldots, X_n).

  1. Suppose X \sim U(\theta, 1). Write down the CDF of X.

  2. Show that the CDF of \hat{\theta} is given by F_{\hat{\theta}}(x) = \begin{cases} 0, & x < \theta, \\ 1 - \left( \dfrac{1 - x}{1 - \theta} \right)^n, & \theta \le x \le 1, \\ 1, & x > 1. \end{cases}

  3. Derive the pdf f_{\hat{\theta}}(x) of \hat{\theta}.

  4. Let Y \sim \mathrm{Beta}(1, n).
    Show that for Z = (1 - \theta)Y + \theta, the pdf of Z is, for \theta < z < 1, f_Z(z) = n \frac{(1 - z)^{n - 1}}{(1 - \theta)^n}, and hence conclude that f_{\hat{\theta}}(x) = f_Z(x) for all x \in \mathbb{R}.

  5. Is \hat{\theta} a consistent estimator of \theta? Justify your answer.

Solution

(1)

The CDF of X \sim U(\theta, 1) is F_X(x) = \begin{cases} 0, & x < \theta, \\ \dfrac{x - \theta}{1 - \theta}, & \theta \le x \le 1, \\ 1, & x > 1. \end{cases}

(2)

\begin{align*} P(\hat{\theta} \le x) &= 1 - P(\hat{\theta} > x) = 1 - P(X_1 > x, \ldots, X_n > x) \\ &= 1 - (1 - F_X(x))^n. \end{align*}

For x \in (\theta, 1): P(\hat{\theta} \le x) = 1 - \left( \frac{1 - x}{1 - \theta} \right)^n.

Hence, F_{\hat{\theta}}(x) = \begin{cases} 0, & x < \theta, \\ 1 - \left( \dfrac{1 - x}{1 - \theta} \right)^n, & \theta \le x \le 1, \\ 1, & x > 1. \end{cases}

(3)

Differentiating the CDF for x \in (\theta, 1): \begin{align*} f_{\hat{\theta}}(x) &= \frac{\partial}{\partial x} F_{\hat{\theta}}(x) = n \frac{(1 - x)^{n - 1}}{(1 - \theta)^n}. \end{align*}

(4)

Let Y \sim \mathrm{Beta}(1, n), and define Z = (1 - \theta)Y + \theta.
Then for z \in (\theta, 1): \begin{align*} F_Z(z) &= P(Z \le z) = P(Y \le (z - \theta)/(1 - \theta)), \\ f_Z(z) &= \frac{1}{1 - \theta} f_Y\left( \frac{z - \theta}{1 - \theta} \right). \end{align*}

Since f_Y(y) = n(1 - y)^{n - 1} for 0 < y < 1, \begin{align*} f_Z(z) &= \frac{1}{1 - \theta} n \left( 1 - \frac{z - \theta}{1 - \theta} \right)^{n - 1} \\ &= n \frac{(1 - z)^{n - 1}}{(1 - \theta)^n}. \end{align*}

Thus, f_Z(z) = f_{\hat{\theta}}(z) for all z \in \mathbb{R}, so \hat{\theta} has the same distribution as Z.

(5)

Since Z \overset{d}{=} \hat{\theta}, we have \mathrm{E}[Z] = \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] and \mathrm{Var}[Z] = \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}].

Bias: \begin{align*} \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] &= \mathrm{E}[Z] = \mathrm{E}[(1 - \theta)Y + \theta] = (1 - \theta)\mathrm{E}[Y] + \theta \\ &= (1 - \theta)\frac{1}{n + 1} + \theta. \end{align*}

Hence, \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta}) = (1 - \theta)\frac{1}{n + 1}.

Variance: \begin{align*} \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}] &= \mathrm{Var}[Z] = (1 - \theta)^2 \mathrm{Var}[Y] = (1 - \theta)^2 \frac{n}{(n + 1)^2 (n + 2)}. \end{align*}

As n \to \infty, both the bias and variance tend to 0: \lim_{n \to \infty} \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta}) = 0, \qquad \lim_{n \to \infty} \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\theta}] = 0.

Therefore, \hat{\theta} is a consistent estimator of \theta.

ImportantQuestion 73: Two-Sample t-Test for Difference in Means

Two regions in a country have taken different approaches to promoting healthy lifestyles.
We are interested in comparing the mean systolic blood pressure (in mm Hg) of adult females in the two regions, denoted by \mu_1 and \mu_2.

Data from small independent random samples of adult females yield the following summary statistics:

Region n_i \bar{x}_i s_i^2
1 9 130.222 23.944
2 8 125.000 45.429

Conduct a two-sample t-test (assuming equal variances) with a two-tailed alternative hypothesis to test
H_0: \mu_1 = \mu_2 \quad \text{against} \quad H_1: \mu_1 \neq \mu_2.

Solution

Step 1: Pooled variance

The pooled variance is
s_{\text{pooled}}^2 = \frac{(n_1 - 1)s_1^2 + (n_2 - 1)s_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2} = \frac{(9 - 1)(23.944) + (8 - 1)(45.429)}{9 + 8 - 2} = 33.9704.

Step 2: Test statistic

The test statistic is
t = \frac{\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2} {s_{\text{pooled}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_1} + \frac{1}{n_2}}} = \frac{130.222 - 125.000} {\sqrt{33.9704}\sqrt{\frac{1}{9} + \frac{1}{8}}} = 1.844.

Step 3: Degrees of freedom

The degrees of freedom are
\nu = n_1 + n_2 - 2 = 15.

Step 4: Decision and conclusion

From t-tables with \nu = 15, 0.05 < p < 0.10. Thus, we fail to reject H_0 at the 5% significance level.

There is insufficient evidence to conclude that the mean systolic blood pressure differs between the two regions.

ImportantQuestion 74: Bayesian Reliability Analysis with a Weibull Model

A reliability engineer is studying the lifespan of a machine part under varying stress conditions.

The engineer collects data from independent machine parts. Each data point T_i represents the time to failure (in hours) of part i under stress level d_i.

The time to failure is assumed to follow a Weibull distribution: T_i \mid \theta \sim \mathrm{Weibull}\!\left(k, (\theta/d_i)^{1/k}\right), \quad k > 0, \, \theta > 0, where d_i>0 is the known stress level for part i, k is the known shape, and \theta is the unknown parameter governing the scale.

  1. Derive the likelihood function for n independent observations.

  2. Suppose the prior for \theta is Inverse Gamma, that is \theta \sim \mathrm{InvGamma}(a, b).

    Show that this prior is conjugate within the Inverse Gamma family.

  3. The engineer records the following time-to-failure data (in hours) and stress levels:

    Stress level (d_i) 1 5 10 15 20
    Time to failure (T_i) 3.1 1.4 0.8 0.6 0.7

    Using k=1, a=20, and b=3, determine the posterior distribution for \theta.

  4. Using the prior and posterior parameters obtained above, complete the following table to summarise the engineer’s updated beliefs about \theta:

    Quantity Prior Posterior
    \mathrm{Mode}(\theta) ? ?
    \mathrm{E}[\theta] ? ?
    \mathrm{SD}(\theta) ? ?
Solution

(1)

For n independent observations, \begin{align*} f(\underline{t} \mid \theta) &= \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{k}{\theta / d_i} t_i^{k - 1} \exp\!\left(-\frac{t_i^k}{\theta / d_i}\right) \\ &= \frac{k^n}{\theta^n} \left( \prod_{i=1}^n d_i t_i^{k - 1} \right) \exp\!\left( -\frac{1}{\theta} \sum_{i=1}^n d_i t_i^k \right). \end{align*}

(2)

The prior is \pi(\theta) \propto \theta^{-(a+1)} e^{-b / \theta}.

Combining with the likelihood: \begin{align*} \pi(\theta \mid \underline{t}) &\propto \theta^{-n} \exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^n d_i t_i^k\right) \times \theta^{-(a+1)} e^{-b / \theta} \\ &\propto \theta^{-(a + n + 1)} \exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{\theta}\bigg[b + \sum_{i=1}^n d_i t_i^k\bigg]\right). \end{align*}

Hence, \theta \mid \underline{t} \sim \mathrm{InvGamma}\!\left(a + n,\, b + \sum_{i=1}^n d_i t_i^k\right).

Since the posterior is also an Inverse Gamma, the prior is conjugate.

(3)

For the given data: \begin{align*} \sum_{i=1}^n d_i t_i &= 1(3.1) + 5(1.4) + 10(0.8) + 15(0.6) + 20(0.7) = 41.1. \end{align*}

With a = 20 and b = 3, we have \theta \mid \underline{t} \sim \mathrm{InvGamma}(25, 44.1).

(4)

For \theta \sim \mathrm{InvGamma}(a, b): \mathrm{Mode}(\theta) = \frac{b}{a + 1}, \quad \mathrm{E}[\theta] = \frac{b}{a - 1}, \quad \mathrm{SD}(\theta) = \frac{b}{a - 1}\sqrt{\frac{1}{a - 2}}.

Thus:

Quantity Prior (a=20,b=3) Posterior (a=25,b=44.1)
\mathrm{Mode}(\theta) 3/21 \approx 0.143 44.1/26 \approx 1.696
\mathrm{E}[\theta] 3/19 \approx 0.158 44.1/24 \approx 1.838
\mathrm{SD}(\theta) \approx 0.037 \approx 0.383

After observing the data, the engineer’s belief shifts toward a larger value of \theta, reflecting an increase in the estimated scale of lifetimes at a given stress level. The posterior standard deviation is larger than the prior standard deviation for these data.

Important 51: Question 75: MLE for the Uniform Distribution U(\theta, 2\theta)

Let X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n be independent and identically distributed random variables from the Uniform distribution: X_i \sim U(\theta, 2\theta), \qquad \theta > 0.

Take the density to be 1/\theta on the closed interval [\theta,2\theta] and zero outside it. Obtain the maximum likelihood estimator \hat{\theta}.

Solution

Step 1: Write down the PDF

The probability density function is
f(x \mid \theta) = \begin{cases} \dfrac{1}{\theta}, & \theta \le x \le 2\theta, \\[6pt] 0, & \text{otherwise.} \end{cases}

Step 2: Construct the likelihood

For a random sample x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n,
L(\theta; \underline{x}) = \prod_{i=1}^n f(x_i \mid \theta) = \begin{cases} \theta^{-n}, & \theta \le \min(x_i), \; 2\theta \ge \max(x_i), \\[4pt] 0, & \text{otherwise.} \end{cases}

This condition can be rewritten as
\frac{1}{2}\max(x_i) \le \theta \le \min(x_i).

Step 3: Identify the MLE

Within this interval, L(\theta) = \theta^{-n}, which is a decreasing function of \theta.
Hence, the likelihood is maximized by taking the smallest allowable value of \theta: \hat{\theta} = \frac{1}{2}\max(X_i).

Step 4: Conclusion

The maximum likelihood estimator is
\boxed{\hat{\theta} = \tfrac{1}{2}\max(X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n).}

ImportantQuestion 76: Bias, Mean Squared Error, and Estimator Comparison

  1. Define the bias and mean squared error (MSE) of an estimator \hat{\theta} of a parameter \theta. Given the bias and variance of an estimator, explain how you would find the mean squared error.

  2. Suppose a random variable X has \mathrm{E}[X] = 2\theta + 1, \quad \mathrm{Var}(X) = 2\theta^2 + 1, and another independent random variable Y has \mathrm{E}[Y] = 3\theta - 2, \quad \mathrm{Var}(Y) = 3\theta^2. Two estimators of \theta are proposed: \hat{\theta}_1 = \frac{3X - Y - 5}{3}, \qquad \hat{\theta}_2 = \frac{2Y - X + 4}{4}. Which of these estimators would you recommend? Justify your answer.

Solution

(a) Definitions

  • Bias:
    \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta}) = \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] - \theta

  • Mean Squared Error (MSE):
    \mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\theta}) = \mathrm{E}[(\hat{\theta} - \theta)^2]

  • Relationship:
    \mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\theta}) = \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}) + \mathrm{Bias}(\hat{\theta})^2

(b) Computation of properties

Estimator \mathrm{E}[\hat{\theta}] \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}) Bias MSE
\hat{\theta}_1 = \dfrac{3X - Y - 5}{3} \theta \dfrac{21\theta^2 + 9}{9} 0 \dfrac{21\theta^2 + 9}{9}
\hat{\theta}_2 = \dfrac{2Y - X + 4}{4} \theta - \tfrac{1}{4} \dfrac{14\theta^2 + 1}{16} -\tfrac{1}{4} \dfrac{14\theta^2 + 2}{16}

Choosing an estimator

Comparing the MSEs: \frac{14}{16} < \frac{21}{9}, \qquad \frac{2}{16} < 1, so \hat{\theta}_2 has the smaller MSE for all values of \theta.

Therefore, \hat{\theta}_2 is preferred.

ImportantQuestion 77: Unbiasedness of the Sample Variance and Pooled Variance; Hypothesis Test for Means

  1. The sample variance for a random sample X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n can be written as
    S^2 = \frac{1}{n-1} \left( \sum_{i=1}^n X_i^2 - n\bar{X}^2 \right). Show that S^2 is unbiased for the population variance \sigma^2.

  2. Suppose we have two independent Normal random samples, of sizes n_1 and n_2, with the same population variance \sigma^2. The pooled variance estimator is S^2_{\text{pooled}} = \frac{(n_1 - 1)S_1^2 + (n_2 - 1)S_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2}, where S_1^2 and S_2^2 are the two sample variances.
    Show that S^2_{\text{pooled}} is unbiased for \sigma^2.

  3. Download speeds (in megabits per second) were measured for customers of two internet providers. The summary statistics were:
    n_1 = 20, \; \bar{x}_1 = 289.3, \; s_1 = 10.8, \qquad n_2 = 10, \; \bar{x}_2 = 298.1, \; s_2 = 15.7. Assume these are independent Normal random samples with equal population variances. Test the hypothesis that the two providers have the same average download speed against a two-sided alternative at the 5\% level. You may use t_{0.975,28}=2.048.

Solution

  1. Unbiasedness of the sample variance

    For any random variable X, \mathrm{Var}(X) = \mathrm{E}[X^2] - (\mathrm{E}[X])^2 \quad \Rightarrow \quad \mathrm{E}[X^2] = \mathrm{Var}(X) + (\mathrm{E}[X])^2. Then, \begin{align*} \mathrm{E}[S^2] &= \frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^n \mathrm{E}[X_i^2] - n \mathrm{E}[\bar{X}^2]\right) \\ &= \frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^n (\sigma^2 + \mu^2) - n\left(\frac{\sigma^2}{n} + \mu^2\right)\right) \\ &= \sigma^2. \end{align*} Hence, S^2 is unbiased for \sigma^2.

  2. Unbiasedness of the pooled variance

    Since each sample variance is unbiased: \mathrm{E}[S_1^2] = \sigma^2 \quad \text{and} \quad \mathrm{E}[S_2^2] = \sigma^2. Thus, \mathrm{E}[(n_1 - 1)S_1^2 + (n_2 - 1)S_2^2] = (n_1 + n_2 - 2)\sigma^2, and dividing both sides by (n_1 + n_2 - 2) gives \mathrm{E}[S^2_{\text{pooled}}] = \sigma^2. Therefore, S^2_{\text{pooled}} is unbiased.

  3. Hypothesis test for equality of means

    Compute the pooled variance: s^2_{\text{pooled}} = \frac{(n_1 - 1)s_1^2 + (n_2 - 1)s_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2} = \frac{19(10.8)^2 + 9(15.7)^2}{28} = 158.38.

    The test statistic is: t = \frac{\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2}{\sqrt{s^2_{\text{pooled}}\left(\frac{1}{n_1} + \frac{1}{n_2}\right)}} = \frac{289.3 - 298.1}{\sqrt{158.38\left(\frac{1}{20} + \frac{1}{10}\right)}} = -1.805.

    Degrees of freedom: n_1 + n_2 - 2 = 28.

    Use the two-sided test: H_0: \mu_1 = \mu_2, \qquad H_1: \mu_1 \neq \mu_2.

    The 5% two-sided critical value is t_{0.975,28}=2.048. Since |t|=1.805<2.048, the result is not significant at the 5% level. Therefore, there is insufficient evidence at the 5% level to conclude that the providers have different mean download speeds.

ImportantQuestion 78: Bernoulli Likelihood, Fisher Information, and Inference

  1. Suppose \underline{X} = (X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n) is a random sample from a Bernoulli distribution with probability mass function
    \Pr(X = x \mid \theta) = \theta^x (1 - \theta)^{1 - x}, \quad x = 0, 1, \; 0 < \theta < 1. Write down the log-likelihood function \ell(\theta ; \underline{x}) and derive the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) \hat{\theta} for \theta when 0<\sum_{i=1}^n x_i<n.

  2. Obtain the Fisher information \mathcal{I}_n(\theta) and hence give the approximate large-sample variance of \hat{\theta}.

  3. Out of a random sample of 200 people from a population, 30 were found to be left-handed.

    Assuming the probability of being left-handed is \theta, obtain an approximate 95\% confidence interval for \theta using the asymptotic distribution of the MLE. You may use z_{0.975}=1.960.

  4. Use a likelihood ratio test at the 5\% level to assess whether there is evidence against the null hypothesis H_0 : \theta = 0.1. You may use the 95th percentile of \chi^2_1, which is 3.841.

  5. The odds parameter \pi is a monotonic transformation of \theta, given by
    \pi = \frac{\theta}{1 - \theta}.

    1. What is the MLE of \pi for the data in part 3? (You do not need to consider the likelihood of \pi, but you should state any property of MLEs used.)
    2. Give an approximate 95\% confidence interval for \pi.
Solution

  1. Log-likelihood and MLE

    The likelihood is L(\theta ; \underline{x}) = \prod_{i=1}^n \theta^{x_i}(1 - \theta)^{1 - x_i} = \theta^{\sum x_i}(1 - \theta)^{n - \sum x_i} = \theta^{n\bar{x}}(1 - \theta)^{n(1 - \bar{x})}.

    Hence the log-likelihood: \ell(\theta ; \underline{x}) = n\bar{x}\log\theta + n(1 - \bar{x})\log(1 - \theta).

    Differentiate: \frac{\partial}{\partial \theta} \ell(\theta;\underline{x}) = \frac{n\bar{x}}{\theta} - \frac{n(1 - \bar{x})}{1 - \theta}. Setting \frac{\partial \ell}{\partial \theta} = 0 gives \hat{\theta} = \bar{x}. The second derivative: \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \theta^2} \ell(\theta;\underline{x}) = -\frac{n\bar{x}}{\theta^2} - \frac{n(1 - \bar{x})}{(1 - \theta)^2}. is negative, so this is indeed a maximum.

  1. Fisher Information and Asymptotic Variance

    We have

    \begin{align*} \mathcal{I}_n(\theta) &= -\mathrm{E}\!\left[\frac{\partial^2}{\partial \theta^2} \ell(\theta;\underline{x})\right] \\ &= \mathrm{E}\left[\frac{n\bar{x}}{\theta^2} + \frac{n(1 - \bar{x})}{(1 - \theta)^2}\right] \\ &= \frac{n\theta}{\theta^2} + \frac{n(1-\theta)}{(1-\theta)^2} \\ &= \frac{n}{\theta(1-\theta)}. \end{align*}

    Hence the asymptotic variance: \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}) = \frac{\theta(1 - \theta)}{n}.

  1. Approximate 95% confidence interval

    From the data: \hat{\theta} = \bar{x} = 30/200 = 0.15.
    Estimated variance: \widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(\hat{\theta}) = \frac{0.15(0.85)}{200} = 0.0006375. Standard error: \mathrm{SE} = \sqrt{0.0006375} = 0.0253.
    Approximate 95% CI: 0.15 \pm 1.96(0.0253) = (0.1005, 0.1995).

  1. Likelihood ratio test

    Log-likelihood function: \ell(\theta \mid x) = n\bar{x}\log\theta + n(1 - \bar{x})\log(1 - \theta) = 30\log\theta + 170\log(1 - \theta).

    Compute: \ell_A = \ell(\hat{\theta} \mid x) = 30\log(0.15) + 170\log(0.85) = -84.542, \\ \ell_B = \ell(0.1 \mid x) = 30\log(0.1) + 170\log(0.9) = -86.989. Test statistic: D = 2(\ell_A - \ell_B) = 4.894. Under H_0, D is approximately \chi^2_1 for large samples. Since 4.894 > 3.841 (the 5% critical value), we reject H_0 at the 5% level.
    There is evidence that the proportion of left-handed people differs from 0.1.

  1. Odds parameter

    1. By the invariance property of MLEs: \hat{\pi} = \frac{\hat{\theta}}{1 - \hat{\theta}} = \frac{0.15}{0.85} = 0.176.

    2. Since the transformation is monotonic, transform the CI for \theta: (0.1005, 0.1995) \mapsto \left( \frac{0.1005}{0.8995}, \frac{0.1995}{0.8005} \right) = (0.112, 0.249).

    Thus, the 95% CI for \pi is (0.112, 0.249).

Important 52: Question 79: Conjugacy: Binomial Model, Beta Prior

Suppose that we observe a single Binomial Binomial observation X \mid p \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n,p) Show that choosing a Beta Beta prior p \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a,b) yields the posterior distribution p \mid x \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a + x, b + n - x).

Solution

We compute the posterior using Bayes theorem: \pi(p \mid x) \propto \pi(p) f(x\mid p).

  1. Prior PDF: Since p \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a,b), its probability density function is \pi(p) = \frac{\Gamma(a+b)}{\Gamma(a) \Gamma(b)} p^{a-1}(1-p)^{b-1}. Ignoring the normalisation constant, this is proportional to \pi(p) \propto p^{a-1}(1-p)^{b-1}.
  2. Likelihood: Since X\mid p \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n, p), the likelihood is f(x \mid p) = \binom{n}{x} p^{x}(1-p)^{n-x}. Dropping the factors that do not depend on p, this is proportional to f(x \mid p) \propto p^{x}(1-p)^{n-x}.
  3. Posterior: Using the prior and likelihood derived above, we have \pi(p \mid x) \propto p^{a-1}(1-p)^{b-1} \times p^{x}(1-p)^{n-x} \propto p^{a + x - 1} (1-p)^{b + n - x}. This takes the form of a Beta density function and thus p \mid x \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a + x, b + n - x), and so the Beta distribution is conjugate 6.3.
Important 53: Question 80: Conjugacy: Poisson Model, Gamma Prior

Suppose that we observe n i.i.d. Poisson Poisson observations X_i \mid \lambda \; \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \; \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda) Show that choosing a Gamma Gamma prior \lambda \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a,b) yields the posterior distribution \lambda \mid \underline{x} \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a + n\bar{x}, b + n).

Solution

We compute the posterior using Bayes theorem: \pi(\lambda \mid \underline{x}) \propto \pi(\lambda) f(\underline{x}\mid\lambda).

  1. Prior PDF: Since \lambda \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a,b), its probability density function is \pi(\lambda) = \frac{b^a}{\Gamma(a)} \lambda^{a-1} e^{-b\lambda}. Ignoring the normalisation constant, this is proportional to \pi(\lambda) \propto \lambda^{a-1} e^{-b\lambda}.
  2. Likelihood: Since we have n i.i.d. observations X_i \mid \lambda \; \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \; \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda), the likelihood is \begin{align*} f(\underline{x} \mid \lambda) &= \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{\lambda^{x_i} e^{-\lambda}}{x_i!} \\ &= \lambda^{n\bar{x}} e^{-n\lambda} \left(\prod_{i=1}^n x_i!\right)^{-1}. \end{align*} Dropping the factors that do not depend on \lambda, this is proportional to f(\underline{x} \mid \lambda) \propto \lambda^{n\bar{x}} e^{-n\lambda}.
  3. Posterior: Using the prior and likelihood derived above, we have \begin{align*} \pi(\lambda \mid \underline{x}) &\propto \lambda^{a-1} e^{-b\lambda} \times \lambda^{n\bar{x}} e^{-n\lambda} \\ &\propto \lambda^{a + n\bar{x} - 1} e^{-(b + n)\lambda}. \end{align*} This takes the form of a Gamma density function and thus \lambda \mid \underline{x} \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a + n\bar{x}, b + n), and so the Gamma distribution is conjugate 6.3.
Important 54: Question 81: Conjugacy: Geometric Model, Beta Prior

Suppose that we observe n i.i.d. Geometric Geometric observations X_i \mid p \; \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \; \mathrm{Geom}(p) Show that choosing a Beta Beta prior p \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a,b) yields the posterior distribution p \mid \underline{x} \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a + n, b + n(\bar{x} - 1)).

Solution

We compute the posterior using Bayes theorem: \pi(p \mid \underline{x}) \propto \pi(p) f(\underline{x}\mid p).

  1. Prior PDF: Since p \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a,b), its probability density function is \pi(p) = \frac{\Gamma(a+b)}{\Gamma(a) \Gamma(b)} p^{a-1}(1-p)^{b-1}. Ignoring the normalisation constant, this is proportional to \pi(p) \propto p^{a-1}(1-p)^{b-1}.
  2. Likelihood: Since we have n i.i.d. observations X_i \mid p \; \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \; \mathrm{Geom}(p), the likelihood is \begin{align*} f(\underline{x} \mid p) &= \prod_{i=1}^n p(1-p)^{x_i-1}\\ &= p^n (1-p)^{n(\bar{x} - 1)}. \end{align*} Note that there are no factors that do not depend on p.
  3. Posterior: Using the prior and likelihood derived above, we have \begin{align*} \pi(p \mid \underline{x}) &\propto p^{a-1}(1-p)^{b-1} \times p^n (1-p)^{n(\bar{x} - 1)} \\ &\propto p^{a + n - 1} (1-p)^{b + n(\bar{x} - 1) - 1}. \end{align*} This takes the form of a Beta density function and thus p \mid \underline{x} \sim \mathrm{Beta}(a + n, b + n(\bar{x} - 1)), and so the Beta distribution is conjugate 6.3.
Important 55: Question 82: Conjugacy: Normal Model with known variance, Normal Prior for mean

Suppose that we observe n i.i.d. Normal Normal observations X_i \mid \mu \; \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \; \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2), where the observation variance \sigma^2 is a known quantity. Show that choosing a Normal Normal prior \mu \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_0, \sigma^2_0) yields the posterior distribution \mu \mid \underline{x} \sim \mathcal{N}(M, V), where M = \frac{\frac{\mu_0}{\sigma^2_0} + \frac{n\bar{x}}{\sigma^2}}{ \frac{1}{\sigma^2_0} + \frac{n}{\sigma^2}}, \qquad V = \frac{1}{\frac{1}{\sigma^2_0} + \frac{n}{\sigma^2}}.

Solution

We compute the posterior using Bayes theorem: \pi(\mu \mid \underline{x}) \propto \pi(\mu) f(\underline{x}\mid \mu).

  1. Prior PDF: Since \mu \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_0, \sigma^2_0), its probability density function is \pi(\mu) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi \sigma^2_0}} \exp\left\{ -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_0}(\mu - \mu_0)^2 \right\} Ignoring the factors which do not depend on \mu, this is proportional to \begin{align*} \pi(\mu) &\propto \exp\left\{ -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_0}(\mu - \mu_0)^2 \right\} \\ &\propto \exp\left\{ -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_0}(\mu^2 - 2\mu \mu_0 + \mu_0^2) \right\} \\ &\propto \exp\left\{ -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_0}(\mu^2 - 2\mu \mu_0) \right\} \exp\left\{ -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_0}( \mu_0^2) \right\} \\ &\propto \exp\left\{ -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_0}(\mu^2 - 2\mu \mu_0) \right\}. \end{align*}
  2. Likelihood: Since we have n i.i.d. observations X_i \mid \mu \; \overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \; \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2), the likelihood is \begin{align*} f(\underline{x} \mid \mu) &= \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}} \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}(x_i - \mu)^2\right\} \\ &= (2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2} \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2\right\} \end{align*} Dropping the factors that do not depend on \mu, this is proportional to \begin{align*} f(\underline{x} \mid \mu) &\propto \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2\right\} \\ &\propto \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n (x_i^2 - 2\mu x_i + \mu^2)\right\} \\ &\propto \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left[ \sum_{i=1}^n \mu^2 - 2\mu \sum_{i=1}^n x_i + \sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2 \right]\right\} \\ &\propto \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left[ n\mu^2 - 2 \, \mu \, n\bar{x} \right]\right\} \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left[ \sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2 \right]\right\} \\ &\propto \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left[ n\mu^2 - 2 \, \mu \, n\bar{x} \right]\right\}. \end{align*}
  3.
