    Using your expression for the posterior mean M and posterior precision T from part (c):

    1. Write down the posterior distribution \mu \mid \underline{x} \sim \mathcal{N}(M,\ 1/T) with the numerical values of M and 1/T.

      [2 marks]

    2. Compute the posterior probability \Pr(\mu < 0 \mid \underline{x}), giving your answer using the standard Normal distribution function \Phi(\cdot) and evaluating it numerically. [2 marks]

You may use the standard Normal CDF values \Phi(2.48) = 0.9934, \Phi(2.49) = 0.9936, and \Phi(2.50) = 0.9938.

Solution

(a) A prior is conjugate to a likelihood when the prior and posterior belong to the same distribution family. This lets us compute the posterior by updating the prior parameters using the data. Here the Normal prior leads to a Normal posterior.

(b)(i) Independence gives f(\underline{x}\mid\mu) = \left(\frac{\tau}{2\pi}\right)^{n/2} \exp\!\left\{-\frac{\tau}{2}\sum_{i=1}^n(x_i-\mu)^2\right\}. Expanding the sum of squares gives \sum_i x_i^2-2n\mu\bar{x}+n\mu^2. Dropping the factors independent of \mu leaves f(\underline{x}\mid\mu)\propto \exp\!\left\{-\frac{n\tau}{2}(\mu^2-2\mu\bar{x})\right\}.

(b)(ii) The prior density is \pi(\mu)=\sqrt{\frac{\tau_0}{2\pi}}\, \exp\!\left\{-\frac{\tau_0}{2}(\mu-\mu_0)^2\right\}.

(c) Multiply the likelihood and the prior, keeping terms that depend on \mu. \pi(\mu\mid\underline{x})\propto \exp\!\left\{-\frac{1}{2} \left[(\tau_0+n\tau)\mu^2-2(\tau_0\mu_0+n\tau\bar{x})\mu\right]\right\}. Set T=\tau_0+n\tau and M=(\tau_0\mu_0+n\tau\bar{x})/T. Then \mu^2-2M\mu=(\mu-M)^2-M^2. Dropping the constant term after completing the square gives the kernel of \mathcal{N}(M,1/T).

(d)(i) Here T=0.01+20(0.04)=0.81 and M=20(0.04)(-2.8)/0.81=-2.24/0.81. The posterior is \mu\mid\underline{x}\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(-\frac{2.24}{0.81},\frac{1}{0.81}\right) \approx \mathcal{N}(-2.765,\,1.235).

(d)(ii) Standardising using the posterior standard deviation 1/\sqrt{T} gives \Pr(\mu<0\mid\underline{x}) =\Phi\bigl((0-M)\sqrt{T}\bigr) =\Phi(2.24/0.9) \approx \Phi(2.49) = 0.9936. Under the model and prior, the posterior probability that the mean reaction time decreased is about 99.4\%.

Important 90: Question 117: Comparing Rate Estimators for Exponential Delivery Times

The Michael Scott Paper Company is analysing its delivery efficiency. Let T_1,\dots,T_n be IID waiting times (in hours) until the next paper delivery is completed, with n>2.

Experience from a rival paper company, Dunder Mifflin, suggests that these waiting times follow an Exponential distribution with unknown rate \lambda>0: T_i \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\lambda), \qquad f(t\mid\lambda)=\lambda e^{-\lambda t},\quad t>0.

Let S_n = \sum_{i=1}^n T_i. It is known that S_n \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(n,\lambda) with density f_{S_n}(s) = \frac{\lambda^n}{\Gamma(n)} s^{n-1} e^{-\lambda s},\qquad s>0.

Ryan, an employee at the Michael Scott Paper Company, proposes the estimator \hat{\lambda}_n = \frac{n}{S_n}.

    1. Using the fact that S_n \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(n,\lambda) and starting from \mathrm{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{S_n}\right] = \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{s} \frac{\lambda^n}{\Gamma(n)} s^{n-1} e^{-\lambda s} \, \mathrm{d}s, show that \mathrm{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{S_n}\right] = \frac{\lambda}{n-1} \int_0^\infty \frac{\lambda^{\,n-1}}{\Gamma(n-1)} s^{n-2} e^{-\lambda s} \, \mathrm{d}s. Hint: You may use the fact that when k is a positive integer \Gamma(k) = (k-1)!. [3 marks]

    2. Hence show that \mathrm{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{S_n}\right] = \frac{\lambda}{n-1}. [2 marks]

    3. Show that \mathrm{E}[\hat{\lambda}_n] = \lambda \cdot \frac{n}{n-1}, and hence state whether \hat{\lambda}_n is biased, and in which direction. [3 marks]

  2. Using the fact that S_n \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(n,\lambda) and starting from \mathrm{E}\!\left[\left(\frac{1}{S_n}\right)^2\right] = \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{s^2} \frac{\lambda^n}{\Gamma(n)} s^{n-1} e^{-\lambda s} \, \mathrm{d}s, show that \mathrm{E}\!\left[\left(\frac{1}{S_n}\right)^2\right] = \frac{\lambda^2}{(n-1)(n-2)} \int_0^\infty \frac{\lambda^{\,n-2}}{\Gamma(n-2)} s^{n-3} e^{-\lambda s} \, \mathrm{d}s, and hence conclude \mathrm{E}\!\left[\left(\frac{1}{S_n}\right)^2\right] = \frac{\lambda^2}{(n-1)(n-2)}, \qquad n>2. [3 marks]

  3. Using the results from parts (a) and (b), compute \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\lambda}_n) and hence show that \hat{\lambda}_n is a consistent estimator of \lambda. [4 marks]

  4. Now consider estimators of \lambda of the form \tilde{\lambda}_n = \frac{a_n}{S_n}, where a_n may depend on n but not on the data.

    1. Find a_n such that \tilde{\lambda}_n is an unbiased estimator of \lambda. [2 marks]

    2. For this choice of a_n compute \mathrm{Var}(\tilde{\lambda}_n), and briefly comment on which estimator, \hat{\lambda} or \tilde{\lambda}, you would recommend and why. [3 marks]

Solution

(a)(i) Multiplying the density by 1/s changes the power of s to n-2. Using \Gamma(n)=(n-1)\Gamma(n-1) gives \mathrm{E}[S_n^{-1}] =\int_0^\infty\frac{\lambda^n}{\Gamma(n)}s^{n-2}e^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}s =\frac{\lambda}{n-1}\int_0^\infty \frac{\lambda^{n-1}}{\Gamma(n-1)}s^{n-2}e^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(a)(ii) The remaining integral is that of a \mathrm{Gamma}(n-1,\lambda) density, so it is 1 and \mathrm{E}[S_n^{-1}]=\lambda/(n-1).

(a)(iii) Hence \mathrm{E}[\hat{\lambda}_n]=n\,\mathrm{E}[S_n^{-1}]=\lambda\frac{n}{n-1}. The bias is \lambda/(n-1)>0, so Ryan’s estimator is biased upwards.

(b) This time the power of s is n-3. Using \Gamma(n)=(n-1)(n-2)\Gamma(n-2) gives \begin{aligned} \mathrm{E}[S_n^{-2}] &=\int_0^\infty\frac{\lambda^n}{\Gamma(n)}s^{n-3}e^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}s\\ &=\frac{\lambda^2}{(n-1)(n-2)}\int_0^\infty \frac{\lambda^{n-2}}{\Gamma(n-2)}s^{n-3}e^{-\lambda s}\,\mathrm{d}s\\ &=\frac{\lambda^2}{(n-1)(n-2)}. \end{aligned} The integral equals 1 because the integrand is the \mathrm{Gamma}(n-2,\lambda) density, which is defined since n>2.

(c) The variance is \begin{aligned} \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\lambda}_n) &=n^2\left(\frac{\lambda^2}{(n-1)(n-2)}-\frac{\lambda^2}{(n-1)^2}\right)\\ &=\frac{n^2\lambda^2}{(n-1)^2(n-2)}. \end{aligned} This tends to zero, and the bias \lambda/(n-1) also tends to zero. Thus the MSE tends to zero and \hat{\lambda}_n converges in probability to \lambda.

(d)(i) Since \mathrm{E}[\tilde{\lambda}_n]=a_n\lambda/(n-1), set a_n=n-1 for unbiasedness.

(d)(ii) With that choice, \mathrm{Var}(\tilde{\lambda}_n)=(n-1)^2\mathrm{Var}(S_n^{-1}) =\frac{\lambda^2}{n-2}. Ryan’s variance is larger by a factor of n^2/(n-1)^2. The estimator (n-1)/S_n has both zero bias and smaller variance, so it has the smaller MSE.

Important 91: Question 118: Frequentist and Bayesian Inference for a Pareto Tail Index

A reliability engineer is studying the size (in kWh) of rare but severe energy surges in a new power-conversion system. Only surges exceeding a safety threshold of x_{\min} = 100 kWh are recorded. Let T_1,\dots,T_n be the observed surge sizes, assumed IID with T_i \mid \alpha \sim \mathrm{Pareto}(x_{\min}, \alpha), \qquad i=1,\dots,n, where the tail index \alpha > 0 is unknown and x_{\min} = 100 is known. The density of T_i is f(t \mid \alpha) = \frac{\alpha x_{\min}^{\alpha}}{t^{\alpha+1}}, \qquad t \ge x_{\min}, \ \alpha > 0.

A sample of n = 40 surges yields observed sizes t_1,\dots,t_{40} with \sum_{i=1}^{40} \log\!\left(\frac{t_i}{x_{\min}}\right) = 20.

    1. Show that the log-likelihood function for \alpha based on the sample \underline{t} can be written as \ell(\alpha; \underline{t}) = n \log \alpha + n \alpha \log x_{\min} - (\alpha + 1)\sum_{i=1}^n \log t_i and hence that (up to an additive constant not depending on \alpha) \ell(\alpha; \underline{t}) = n \log \alpha - \alpha \sum_{i=1}^n \log\!\left(\frac{t_i}{x_{\min}}\right) + \text{constant}. [4 marks]

    2. Derive the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) \hat{\alpha} and evaluate it numerically for the given data. [3 marks]

    1. Show that the second derivative of the log-likelihood is \frac{\partial^2 }{\partial \alpha^2} \ell(\alpha; \underline{t})= -\frac{n}{\alpha^2}. Hence compute the Fisher information for n observations, \mathcal{I}_n(\alpha). [2 marks]

    2. Using your result for \mathcal{I}_n(\alpha), obtain the asymptotic distribution of the MLE \hat{\alpha} and write down an approximate 95\% confidence interval for \alpha, expressing your answer in terms of \hat{\alpha}. [3 marks]

  3. Engineers believe, based on past data from similar systems, that the tail index \alpha is likely to be around 2, but with moderate uncertainty.

    They adopt a Gamma prior for \alpha: \alpha \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a_0, b_0), with density \pi(\alpha) \propto \alpha^{a_0 - 1} e^{-b_0 \alpha}, \qquad \alpha > 0.

    1. Show that the choice a_0 = 4 and b_0 = 2 is consistent with the prior belief that \alpha is likely to be near 2, with moderate uncertainty. [2 marks]

    2. Using the prior in part (c)(i) and the given data, derive the posterior distribution of \alpha. Identify the posterior as a Gamma distribution and state its mean and variance.

      Hint: First show that the likelihood can be written in the form f(\underline{t} \mid \alpha) \propto \alpha^{\,n} \exp\!\left\{-\alpha \sum_{i=1}^n \log\!\left(\frac{t_i}{x_{\min}}\right)\right\}.

      [6 marks]

You may use the standard Normal quantile z_{0.975} = 1.960.

Solution

(a)(i) The likelihood for the observed values is L(\alpha;\underline{t}) =\prod_{i=1}^n\frac{\alpha x_{\min}^\alpha}{t_i^{\alpha+1}} =\alpha^n x_{\min}^{n\alpha}\prod_{i=1}^n t_i^{-(\alpha+1)}. Set S=\sum_i\log(t_i/x_{\min}). Taking logs and collecting the terms in \alpha yields \begin{aligned} \ell(\alpha;\underline{t}) &=n\log\alpha+n\alpha\log x_{\min}-(\alpha+1)\sum_i\log t_i\\ &=n\log\alpha-\alpha S-\sum_i\log t_i. \end{aligned}

(a)(ii) The score is n/\alpha-S. It vanishes at \hat{\alpha}=n/S when S>0. The log-likelihood is strictly concave for \alpha>0, so this is its maximum. Here \hat{\alpha}=40/20=2.

(b)(i) Differentiating the score gives \ell''(\alpha;\underline{t})=-n/\alpha^2. This is independent of the observations, so its negative expectation is \mathcal{I}_n(\alpha)=n/\alpha^2.

(b)(ii) The large-sample approximation for the MLE is \hat{\alpha}\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\alpha,\alpha^2/n). Substituting \hat{\alpha} for the unknown \alpha in the standard error gives the approximate 95\% interval \hat{\alpha}\pm 1.960\frac{\hat{\alpha}}{\sqrt n}. For the given sample this is 2\pm 0.620, or approximately (1.38,2.62).

(c)(i) The prior mean is 4/2=2 and the variance is 4/2^2=1. The prior is centred around 2 with standard deviation 1.

(c)(ii) From part (a), the likelihood is proportional to \alpha^n e^{-\alpha S}. Multiplying by the Gamma prior gives \pi(\alpha\mid\underline{t}) \propto \alpha^{a_0+n-1} e^{-(b_0+S)\alpha}. Thus \alpha\mid\underline{t}\sim\mathrm{Gamma}(4+40,2+20)=\mathrm{Gamma}(44,22) in shape–rate notation. Its mean is 44/22=2 and its variance is 44/22^2\approx 0.0909.

Important 92: Question 119: Large-Sample Testing and Power for a Bird Visit Rate

A team of ecologists is studying whether installing small feeders changes the activity of woodland birds. Long-term monitoring has shown that, without feeders, the mean number of bird visits per observation period is \lambda_0.

After installing a feeder, the ecologists record the number of bird visits in each of n observation periods.

Let Y_1, Y_2, \ldots, Y_n denote these counts, assumed IID with Y_i\sim \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda), \qquad i = 1,\dots,n, where \lambda is the true mean number of visits per period.

  1. Explain what it means to “estimate \lambda’’ in the frequentist framework, and how uncertainty about \lambda is represented in the Bayesian framework. [2 marks]

  2. The ecologists want to test whether the feeder changes bird activity, using H_0 : \lambda = \lambda_0 \qquad \text{vs.} \qquad H_1 : \lambda \neq \lambda_0. Because \lambda is unknown and the data are Poisson, they decide to use a large-sample Central Limit Theorem (CLT) approximation to construct a test.

    1. Define the size (significance level) of a hypothesis test. [2 marks]

    2. Define the rejection region of a size-\alpha test. [2 marks]

    3. Let \bar{Y} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n Y_i. Using the CLT, show that under H_0, \sqrt{n}\,\frac{\bar{Y} - \lambda_0}{\sqrt{\lambda_0}} \ \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\ \mathcal{N}(0,1) \qquad\text{for large } n. Hence show that a size-\alpha two-sided test rejects H_0 when \bar{Y} < \lambda_0 - k_\alpha \quad \text{or} \quad \bar{Y} > \lambda_0 + k_\alpha, where k_\alpha > 0 is chosen appropriately. [5 marks]

    4. For the rejection region obtained in part (b)(iii), derive the approximate power function \beta(\lambda) = \Pr(\text{Reject } H_0 \mid \lambda), and express your answer in terms of the standard Normal CDF \Phi(\cdot). [5 marks]

  3. In a particular study, the ecologists take n = 25 observation periods. They wish to test H_0 : \lambda = 5 \qquad \text{vs.} \qquad H_1 : \lambda \neq 5 using the CLT-based test from part (b) at the 1\% significance level.

    Over the 25 periods, the total number of bird visits recorded is \sum_{i=1}^{25} Y_i = 175.

    Using the approximate test derived in part (b):

    1. compute the observed value of the test statistic;

    2. state the critical value(s) for a two-sided test at the 1\% level;

    3. determine whether H_0 should be rejected, and briefly interpret your conclusion in the context of the study.

    [4 marks]

You may use the standard Normal quantile z_{0.995} = 2.576.

Solution

(a) In frequentist inference, \lambda is fixed and unknown. We estimate it with a statistic such as \bar{Y} and use the statistic’s sampling distribution to quantify uncertainty over repeated samples. In Bayesian inference, the posterior distribution of \lambda, formed by updating a prior using the data, represents uncertainty about its value.

(b)(i) With this simple null hypothesis, the size is the probability of rejecting H_0 when \lambda=\lambda_0, the Type I error probability.

(b)(ii) The rejection region is the set of sample outcomes for which the test rejects H_0. For a test of size \alpha, that set has probability \alpha under H_0.

(b)(iii) The Poisson model gives \mathrm{E}[\bar{Y}]=\lambda and \mathrm{Var}(\bar{Y})=\lambda/n. Setting \lambda=\lambda_0 in the CLT gives Z_0=\frac{\bar{Y}-\lambda_0}{\sqrt{\lambda_0/n}} \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1). A two-sided test of approximate size \alpha rejects for |Z_0|>z_{1-\alpha/2}. Equivalently, it rejects when \bar{Y}<\lambda_0-k_\alpha or \bar{Y}>\lambda_0+k_\alpha, where k_\alpha=z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\lambda_0/n}.

(b)(iv) At the true rate \lambda>0, use the approximation \bar{Y}\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\lambda,\lambda/n). Standardising the two rejection tails with this mean and variance gives \begin{aligned} \beta(\lambda)&\approx \Phi\!\left(\frac{\lambda_0-k_\alpha-\lambda}{\sqrt{\lambda/n}}\right)\\ &\quad+1-\Phi\!\left(\frac{\lambda_0+k_\alpha-\lambda}{\sqrt{\lambda/n}}\right). \end{aligned}

(c)(i) The sample mean is 175/25=7, so the observed statistic is (7-5)/\sqrt{5/25}=10/\sqrt{5}\approx 4.47.

(c)(ii) The 1\% two-sided critical values are -2.576 and 2.576.

(c)(iii) Since 4.47>2.576, reject H_0. There is evidence that the mean number of visits differs from five per period. The observed mean suggests a higher visit rate after installing the feeder.

Important 93: Question 120: Bias and Consistency of Gift-Value Estimators

At his eleventy-first (111th) birthday party, Bilbo Baggins distributes many small gifts to the hobbits of the Shire. Let X_1,\ldots,X_n denote the values (in silver pennies) that guests place on their gifts, where the X_i are modelled as IID with mean \mu and variance \sigma^2.

A young hobbit proposes the estimator \hat{\mu}_1 = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i, while another suggests the alternative \hat{\mu}_2 = \frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i.

  1. Show that \hat{\mu}_1 is an unbiased estimator of \mu, and determine the bias of \hat{\mu}_2. [3 marks]

  2. Compute \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mu}_1) and \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\mu}_2). [3 marks]

  3. For each estimator, compute the mean squared error (MSE). If the true mean were \mu=0, which estimator has the smaller MSE for finite n? [2 marks]

  4. State, with a brief justification, whether \hat{\mu}_1 and \hat{\mu}_2 are consistent estimators of \mu. [2 marks]

Solution

  1. By linearity, the means are \mathrm{E}[\hat\mu_1]=\mu and \mathrm{E}[\hat\mu_2]=n\mu/(n+1). Therefore \hat\mu_1 is unbiased and the bias of \hat\mu_2 is -\mu/(n+1).

  2. Independence gives \mathrm{Var}(\sum_{i=1}^n X_i)=n\sigma^2. Dividing by the squared denominators gives \mathrm{Var}(\hat\mu_1)=\frac{\sigma^2}{n}, \qquad \mathrm{Var}(\hat\mu_2)=\frac{n\sigma^2}{(n+1)^2}.

  3. Adding variance and squared bias gives \mathrm{MSE}(\hat\mu_1)=\frac{\sigma^2}{n}, \qquad \mathrm{MSE}(\hat\mu_2)=\frac{n\sigma^2+\mu^2}{(n+1)^2}. When \mu=0, the second MSE is the first multiplied by (n/(n+1))^2, so \hat\mu_2 has the smaller MSE if \sigma^2>0. Both MSEs are zero in the degenerate case \sigma^2=0.

  4. Both MSEs tend to zero as n\to\infty. For any \varepsilon>0 the probability that either estimator differs from \mu by more than \varepsilon is bounded by its MSE divided by \varepsilon^2, so both estimators are consistent.

Important 94: Question 121: Likelihood Ratio Test for a Change in Message Rate

For years, Gandalf has sent messages to the Shire at a rate of once per week (\theta_0 = 1). However, Frodo suspects that the frequency of these messages has changed recently. He records the time intervals T_1, \dots, T_n (in weeks) between the last n+1 consecutive messages he received.

Suppose these time intervals are modelled as independent exponential random variables with density f(t \mid \theta) = \theta e^{-\theta t},\qquad t>0, where \theta > 0 is the unknown rate. Frodo wishes to test the historical rate against a change: H_0 : \theta = 1 \qquad \text{vs.} \qquad H_1 : \theta \neq 1, using the likelihood ratio test.

  1. Write down the log-likelihood \ell(\theta; \underline{t}) for the sample T_1,\dots,T_n, and hence show that the maximum likelihood estimator is \hat{\theta} = \frac{n}{S}, \qquad \text{where } S = \sum_{i=1}^n T_i. [3 marks]

  2. Show that the likelihood ratio \lambda = \frac{L(1; \underline{t})}{L(\hat{\theta};\underline{t})} may be written in the form: \lambda = \left(\frac{1}{\hat{\theta}}\right)^n \exp\!\big(- (1 - \hat{\theta}) S\big). Hence, explain why a rejection region \{\lambda < c\} corresponds to values of \hat{\theta} that are significantly different from 1. [4 marks]

  3. Frodo records n = 5 intervals with a total duration of S = 28 weeks.

    Compute the likelihood ratio \lambda (you may leave your answer in the form A e^{B}).

    [1 mark]

  4. Without computing any critical value or test threshold, use the rule of thumb that values of \lambda close to 1 give little evidence against H_0, while values close to 0 give stronger evidence against H_0.

    Based on the value of \lambda you obtained in part (c), what conclusion should Frodo draw? [2 marks]

Solution

  1. Write S=\sum_{i=1}^n t_i for the observed total. Independence gives L(\theta;\underline t)=\theta^n e^{-\theta S}, hence \ell(\theta;\underline t)=n\log\theta-\theta S. Its derivative n/\theta-S vanishes at \hat\theta=n/S and its second derivative is negative for every \theta>0, establishing the maximum.

  2. Substituting into the ratio gives \lambda=\frac{e^{-S}}{\hat\theta^n e^{-\hat\theta S}} =\left(\frac{1}{\hat\theta}\right)^n\exp\!\big(-(1-\hat\theta)S\big). To see how \lambda varies, set r=S/n=1/\hat\theta. Then \log\lambda=n(\log r+1-r), whose derivative with respect to r is n(1/r-1). The ratio reaches its maximum of 1 at r=1 and decreases as r moves away from 1 on either side. Thus small \lambda corresponds to estimates away from the null rate in either direction.

  3. With n=5 and S=28, the observed estimate is 5/28 and \lambda=\left(\frac{28}{5}\right)^5 e^{-23}\approx5.65\times10^{-7}.

  4. This ratio is close to zero, so the observed intervals provide strong evidence that the rate has changed. The estimate 5/28 messages per week suggests that messages arrive less frequently than the historical rate of one per week.

Important 95: Question 122: A Confidence Interval and Test for a Poisson Sighting Rate

At the Council of Elrond, scouts bring reports from the lands around Isengard. For each of n consecutive days, riders from Rohan record the number of enemy war-parties seen near the Gap of Rohan. Let X_i denote the count on day i, and suppose X_1,\ldots,X_n \stackrel{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim} \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda), where \lambda > 0 is the (unknown) mean number of sightings per day.

In earlier, quieter times, the rate was believed to be about \lambda = 1 sighting per day. The Council now wishes to assess whether enemy activity has increased.

  1. Show that the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) of \lambda is \hat{\lambda} = \bar{X} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i. Compute \mathrm{E}[\hat{\lambda}] and \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\lambda}). [3 marks]

    1. Using the Central Limit Theorem (CLT), state an approximate distribution for \hat{\lambda} when n is large. [2 marks]

    2. Hence write down an approximate 100(1-\alpha)\% confidence interval for \lambda in terms of \hat{\lambda}, n and the normal quantile z_{1-\alpha/2}. [2 marks]

  3. In one set of reports, the scouts observe n = 36 days and a total of \sum_{i=1}^{36} x_i = 90 enemy war-parties near the Gap of Rohan.

    Using the CLT approximation, test H_0:\lambda = 1 against the one-sided alternative H_1:\lambda > 1 at the 5% significance level.

    State clearly whether H_0 should be rejected, and briefly interpret your conclusion in the context of the safety of the route past Isengard. [3 marks]

You may use the standard Normal quantile z_{0.95} = 1.645.

Solution

  1. By independence, the log-likelihood is \ell(\lambda;\underline x)=-n\lambda+\left(\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\right)\log\lambda -\sum_{i=1}^n\log(x_i!). If the sample total is positive, setting the derivative -n+\big(\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\big)/\lambda to zero gives the MLE \hat\lambda=\bar X. If all counts are zero, \bar X=0 gives the boundary estimate in the usual model allowing \lambda=0. In the strictly positive parameter space the likelihood has its supremum as \lambda\downarrow0. Using the Poisson mean and variance and independence gives \mathrm{E}[\bar X]=\lambda,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(\bar X)=\frac{\lambda}{n}.

    1. By the CLT, \hat\lambda is approximately \mathcal N(\lambda,\lambda/n) for large n.

    2. Replacing \lambda by \hat\lambda in the standard error gives the approximate 100(1-\alpha)\% confidence interval \hat\lambda\ \pm\ z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{\hat\lambda}{n}}.

  3. Here \hat\lambda=90/36=2.5. Under H_0, the CLT gives Z=(\hat\lambda-1)/\sqrt{1/36}\overset{\mathrm{approx.}}{\sim}\mathcal N(0,1). The observed z is (2.5-1)/(1/6)=9. Since 9>1.645, reject H_0 at the 5% level. The data support an increase in the mean rate of sightings near the route, suggesting greater danger to travellers.

Important 96: Question 123: Likelihood and Bayesian Inference for Negative Binomial Counts

In the garden of Lothlórien, Galadriel invites Frodo to gaze into her Mirror. The Mirror displays a continuous sequence of visions. Due to the shifting nature of time, any single vision is either a Vision of the Future (with unknown probability p) or a fleeting Reflection of the Past (with probability 1-p).

Frodo watches the Mirror until he has witnessed exactly r Visions of the Future. Let X denote the number of Reflections of the Past he observes before the r-th Vision of the Future appears. The distribution of X is modelled as X \sim \mathrm{NegBin}(r,p), with probability mass function \Pr(X = x \mid p) = \binom{x + r - 1}{r - 1} p^r (1-p)^x, \qquad x = 0,1,2,\ldots, where 0 < p < 1. It can be shown that \mathrm{E}[X] = \frac{r(1-p)}{p}, \qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac{r(1-p)}{p^2}.

Galadriel asks Frodo to repeat this trial on n different occasions (assume these attempts are independent). On each occasion, Frodo watches the Mirror until he has witnessed r Visions of the Future, and records X_i, the number of Reflections of the Past observed before the r-th Vision appears. The resulting observations are X_1,\ldots,X_n. Assume at least one is positive.

  1. Show that the maximum likelihood estimator \hat{p} of p is given by \hat{p} = \frac{r}{r + \bar{X}}, \qquad \text{where } \bar{X} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i. [6 marks]

  2. For a single observation X \sim \mathrm{NegBin}(r,p), show that the second derivative of the log-likelihood (with respect to p) can be written as \frac{\partial^2}{\partial p^2} \log f(X \mid p) = -\,\frac{X}{(1-p)^2} - \frac{r}{p^2}.

    Hence show that the Fisher information for a single observation is \mathcal{I}_1(p) = \frac{r}{p^2(1-p)}, and deduce the Fisher information \mathcal{I}_n(p) for the full sample. [5 marks]

  3. Galadriel possesses ancient wisdom regarding the clarity of the Mirror. She places a \mathrm{Beta}(\alpha,\beta) prior on p, with density \pi(p) \propto p^{\alpha-1}(1-p)^{\beta-1}, \qquad 0 < p < 1.

    1. Show that the posterior distribution for p given the data x_1,\dots,x_n is p \mid x_1,\dots,x_n \sim \mathrm{Beta}\big(\alpha + nr,\; \beta + S\big), \qquad \text{where } S = \sum_{i=1}^n x_i. [4 marks]

    2. Hence write down the posterior mean and variance of p.

      Using your expression for \mathcal{I}_n(p) from part (b), derive the asymptotic posterior distribution for p.

      Comment on the connection between the Bayesian posterior and the frequentist large-sample distribution of the MLE. [5 marks]

Solution

(a) Let S=\sum_i x_i. By independence, the likelihood is L(p;\underline{x}) =p^{nr}(1-p)^S\prod_{i=1}^n\binom{x_i+r-1}{r-1}. Its log has derivative nr/p-S/(1-p). Equating this to zero gives the estimate nr/(nr+S). Because S>0, the solution is interior, and strict concavity gives a maximum. Writing the result in terms of the random sample yields \hat{p}=\frac{r}{r+\bar{X}}.

(b) For a single observation the terms in the log-likelihood that depend on p are r\log p+X\log(1-p). Differentiating twice gives -r/p^2-X/(1-p)^2. Using the stated mean of X, the information is \mathcal{I}_1(p)=\frac{r}{p^2}+\frac{\mathrm{E}[X]}{(1-p)^2} =\frac{r}{p^2}+\frac{r}{p(1-p)} =\frac{r}{p^2(1-p)}. The log-likelihoods add over independent observations, so \mathcal{I}_n(p)=nr/[p^2(1-p)].

(c)(i) Multiplying likelihood and prior gives \pi(p\mid\underline{x})\propto p^{\alpha+nr-1}(1-p)^{\beta+S-1}. The posterior is therefore \mathrm{Beta}(a,b) with a=\alpha+nr and b=\beta+S.

(c)(ii) The posterior mean and variance are \mathrm{E}[p\mid\underline{x}]=\frac{a}{a+b}, \qquad \mathrm{Var}(p\mid\underline{x})=\frac{ab}{(a+b)^2(a+b+1)}. The prior parameters are fixed, so for large n the posterior mean a/(a+b) is close to \hat{p}=nr/(nr+S) and a+b+1 is close to nr+S=nr/\hat{p}. Writing the posterior variance as [a/(a+b)][b/(a+b)]/(a+b+1) gives a leading term of \hat{p}^{\,2}(1-\hat{p})/(nr), the inverse Fisher information at \hat{p}. The resulting Normal approximation is p\mid\underline{x}\overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}\!\left(\hat{p},\,\frac{\hat{p}^{\,2}(1-\hat{p})}{nr}\right). In the frequentist sampling distribution, \hat{p} is random and centred approximately at the true p_0, with approximate variance p_0^2(1-p_0)/(nr). Both approximations use inverse Fisher information. The posterior treats the data as fixed and uses \hat p for its centre and variance calculation.

Important 97: Question 124: Gamma–Poisson Updating for Orc Sightings

On the eastern bank of the Anduin, beyond the borders of Gondor, Faramir’s Rangers observe raiding parties of orcs moving along the river. Let X_i denote the number of orcs sighted in the ith raiding party (crossing a fixed stretch of riverbank) during a scouting mission. Suppose that, conditional on the unknown average orc intensity \lambda > 0, X_1,\ldots,X_n \mid \lambda \stackrel{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim} \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda).

The Rangers, trained in the interpretation of scouting reports, model \lambda using a conjugate prior, \lambda \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_0,\beta_0).

    1. Write down the likelihood L(\lambda; \underline{x}) and show that L(\lambda; \underline{x}) \propto \lambda^{n\bar{x}} e^{-n\lambda}. [3 marks]

    2. Using the prior \lambda \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_0,\beta_0), show that the posterior distribution takes the form \lambda \mid \underline{x} \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_0 + n\bar{x}, \, \beta_0+n). [3 marks]

  2. Across n = 10 observed raiding parties, the Rangers record a total of 243 orcs: \sum_{i=1}^n x_i = 243. Suppose the Rangers choose prior parameters \alpha_0 = 3 and \beta_0 = 0.25.

    1. Write down the posterior distribution of \lambda \mid \underline{x} and compute the posterior mean. [2 marks]

    2. Compute the posterior variance \mathrm{Var}(\lambda \mid \underline{x}) under the posterior from part (b)(i). Comment briefly on whether the captains of Gondor are justified in judging that the western bank is in grave danger if the expected orc count per raiding party exceeds 15. [3 marks]

  3. Denethor, Steward of Gondor, refuses to accept growing signs of danger. Influenced by dark visions from the palantír, he insists that even after receiving the Rangers’ reports, the expected orc count per raiding party is at most 15, that is \mathrm{E}[\lambda \mid \underline{x}] \le 15.

    Assume Denethor also uses a Gamma prior, \lambda \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_D,\beta_D), but with unknown parameters \alpha_D,\beta_D > 0.

    Using the form of the posterior from part (a)(ii), derive a condition on (\alpha_D,\beta_D) which must hold if \mathrm{E}[\lambda \mid \underline{x}] \le 15 under Denethor’s posterior. [5 marks]

  4. Briefly explain how Denethor’s prior \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_D,\beta_D) must differ from the Rangers’ prior \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_0,\beta_0) to maintain such denial after seeing the same evidence. [4 marks]

Solution

(a)(i) The Poisson likelihood for independent observations is L(\lambda;\underline{x}) =\prod_{i=1}^n\frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{x_i}}{x_i!} =\frac{\lambda^{n\bar{x}} e^{-n\lambda}}{\prod_{i=1}^n x_i!} \propto \lambda^{n\bar{x}}e^{-n\lambda}.

(a)(ii) Using the Gamma prior with shape \alpha_0 and rate \beta_0, the posterior kernel is \pi(\lambda\mid\underline{x})\propto \lambda^{\alpha_0+n\bar{x}-1}e^{-(\beta_0+n)\lambda}. Thus \lambda\mid\underline{x}\sim\mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_0+n\bar{x},\beta_0+n).

(b)(i) The updated parameters are 3+243=246 and 0.25+10=10.25. The posterior is \mathrm{Gamma}(246,10.25) with mean 246/10.25=24.

(b)(ii) The posterior variance is 246/10.25^2\approx 2.341. The posterior mean count of 24 exceeds the threshold of 15 and the posterior standard deviation is about 1.53. The captains’ assessment is supported by these data and the Rangers’ prior.

(c) Denethor’s posterior is \mathrm{Gamma}(\alpha_D+243,\beta_D+10). Its mean satisfies \frac{\alpha_D+243}{\beta_D+10}\le 15 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad 15\beta_D-\alpha_D\ge 93.

(d) Write m_D=\alpha_D/\beta_D for Denethor’s prior mean. The condition becomes \beta_D(15-m_D)\ge 93. He needs a prior mean below 15 and a prior weight \beta_D of at least 93/(15-m_D) to offset the data. Since m_D>0, this requires \beta_D>6.2, compared with the Rangers’ weight of 0.25. For example, he could share their prior mean of 12 by taking \beta_D=31 and \alpha_D=372, which gives a posterior mean of exactly 15.

Important 98: Question 125: Sequential Beta–Bernoulli Updating for Riddle Outcomes

Deep in the Misty Mountains, Gollum challenges Bilbo Baggins to a game of riddles. For each riddle t=1,2,3, define X_t = \begin{cases} 1, & \text{if Bilbo answers riddle } t \text{ correctly},\\[3pt] 0, & \text{if he answers incorrectly}. \end{cases}

From Gollum’s perspective, Bilbo has an unknown riddle-ability \theta \in (0,1), and \Pr(X_t = 1 \mid \theta) = \theta, \qquad t=1,2,3, with the riddle outcomes independent given \theta.

Gollum, believing Bilbo to be unlikely to survive a riddle contest, chooses the prior \theta \sim \mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_0,\beta_0), \qquad \alpha_0 = 1,\;\beta_0 = 9.

He then observes the outcomes of three riddles:

  • Riddle 1 (fairly easy): Bilbo answers correctly (X_1 = 1).

  • Riddle 2 (somewhat tricky): Bilbo answers correctly (X_2 = 1).

  • Riddle 3 (a very difficult riddle): Bilbo answers correctly (X_3 = 1).

  1. Compute the prior mean of \theta under the \mathrm{Beta}(1,9) distribution and briefly interpret this from Gollum’s perspective. [2 marks]

  2. Using the Beta–Bernoulli conjugate update, derive Gollum’s posterior distribution for \theta:

    1. after observing X_1 = 1 only;

    2. after observing X_1 = 1 and X_2 = 1;

    3. after observing all three riddles: X_1 = 1, X_2 = 1, X_3 = 1.

    Give each posterior in \mathrm{Beta}(\alpha,\beta) form with updated parameters. [6 marks]

  3. Although Gollum views Riddle 3 as far more difficult than the others, the Beta–Bernoulli model treats all riddles identically. Briefly comment on this modelling assumption: does the model adequately represent Gollum’s beliefs about riddle difficulty? [2 marks]

Solution

  1. The prior mean is 1/(1+9)=0.10. Before seeing the answers, Gollum expects Bilbo to have a 10% chance of answering any given riddle correctly under his model.

  2. With k successes in m independent riddles, the likelihood kernel is \theta^k(1-\theta)^{m-k}. Multiplying by the \mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_0,\beta_0) prior density gives \pi(\theta\mid\underline x) \propto \theta^{\alpha_0+k-1}(1-\theta)^{\beta_0+m-k-1}. Hence each correct answer adds one to the first shape parameter and each incorrect answer adds one to the second. All the answers here are correct.

    1. \theta\mid X_1=1\sim\mathrm{Beta}(2,9).

    2. \theta\mid X_1=X_2=1\sim\mathrm{Beta}(3,9).

    3. \theta\mid X_1=X_2=X_3=1\sim\mathrm{Beta}(4,9).

  3. The model gives every riddle the same conditional success probability \theta and therefore gives every correct answer the same weight. Representing Gollum’s belief about difficulty would require the success probability to depend on the riddle as well as Bilbo’s ability.

Important 99: Question 126: Gaussian Likelihood Ratio Test for a Trend in Echo Intensity

As the Fellowship travels through the Mines of Moria, Gandalf periodically halts the Company to listen for distant echoes of orc movement. At each listening point i = 1,\ldots,n, taken t_i hours after entering the mines, he assigns a numerical intensity Y_i to the sound he hears.

Because echo measurements fluctuate randomly, Gandalf models them as Y_i \sim \mathcal{N}(\beta t_i,\, \sigma^2), independently, where \sigma^2 is known and \beta is an unknown constant. Here \beta represents the rate of change of echo intensity over time.

Gandalf wishes to test whether the echoes are changing in intensity: H_0 : \beta = 0 \qquad\text{vs.}\qquad H_1 : \beta \neq 0.

  1. Write down the likelihood function L(\beta) and the log-likelihood \ell(\beta). By differentiating \ell(\beta) and setting the derivative to zero, show that the maximum likelihood estimator of \beta is \hat{\beta} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n t_i Y_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n t_i^2}. [6 marks]

  2. The likelihood ratio statistic is \Lambda = \frac{L(\beta=0)}{L(\hat{\beta})}.

    1. Show that -2\log\Lambda = \frac{1}{\sigma^2} \left( \sum_{i=1}^n Y_i^2 - \sum_{i=1}^n (Y_i - \hat{\beta} t_i)^2 \right). [4 marks]

    2. State the approximate sampling distribution of -2\log\Lambda under H_0 (you may quote the likelihood ratio theorem), and hence give an approximate size-\alpha rejection region. [3 marks]

  3. During one stretch of tunnels, the Fellowship takes n=10 measurements at times t_i = 0,1,2,\dots,9, using a known \sigma^2 = 1. Gandalf computes \hat{\beta} \approx 0.1373, \qquad -2\log\Lambda = 5.37.

    At the 5\% significance level, carry out the test and state whether the Fellowship should reject H_0. [4 marks]

  4. Briefly interpret your conclusion in the context of the journey through Moria. What would a non-zero \beta indicate about the dangers ahead? [3 marks]

You may use the chi-squared quantile \chi^2_{1,0.95} = 3.841.

Solution

(a) By independence, the likelihood and log-likelihood for observed values y_i are \begin{aligned} L(\beta)&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2} \exp\!\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i-\beta t_i)^2\right\},\\ \ell(\beta)&=-\frac{n}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2) -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i-\beta t_i)^2. \end{aligned} Differentiating gives \ell'(\beta)=\sigma^{-2}\sum_i t_i(y_i-\beta t_i). Let D=\sum_i t_i^2. With D>0, setting the derivative to zero gives \hat{\beta}=\sum_i t_iY_i/D. Since \ell''(\beta)=-D/\sigma^2<0, this is a maximum.

(b)(i) The log likelihood ratio is \log\Lambda=\ell(0)-\ell(\hat{\beta}). The constant cancels, giving -2\log\Lambda =2\{\ell(\hat{\beta})-\ell(0)\} =\frac{1}{\sigma^2} \left[\sum_iY_i^2-\sum_i(Y_i-\hat{\beta}t_i)^2\right].

(b)(ii) The likelihood ratio theorem gives an approximate \chi_1^2 null distribution because the restriction \beta=0 removes one parameter. Reject H_0 when -2\log\Lambda>\chi^2_{1,1-\alpha}. In this model the null distribution is in fact exact. Expanding the square in part (b)(i) and using \sum_i t_iY_i=D\hat{\beta} gives -2\log\Lambda=D\hat{\beta}^{\,2}/\sigma^2. Under H_0, \hat{\beta} is Normal with mean zero and variance \sigma^2/D, so the statistic is the square of a standard Normal variable.

(c) With D=\sum_{t=0}^{9}t^2=285 and \sigma^2=1, the statistic is 285(0.1373)^2\approx 5.37. Since 5.37>3.841, reject H_0 at the 5\% level.

(d) The data provide evidence that the mean echo intensity changes with time. The positive estimated slope indicates intensifying echoes, which could signal approaching orcs and increasing danger. A negative slope would indicate fading echoes.

Important 100: Question 127: Correcting Bias in a Bernoulli Variance Estimate

A quality inspector records X_i=1 if the thickness of panel i exceeds a tolerance and X_i=0 otherwise. Suppose X_1,\ldots,X_n are independent \mathrm{Bernoulli}(p) variables, with 0<p<1 and n\ge 2. The inspector wishes to estimate the population variance v=p(1-p) and considers T=\bar X(1-\bar X).

  1. Find \mathrm{E}[T] and the bias of T as an estimator of v.
  2. Find a constant c_n depending only on n such that c_nT is unbiased for v.
  3. Use X_i^2=X_i to show that c_nT equals the usual sample variance S^2=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^n(X_i-\bar X)^2.
Solution

  1. Independence gives \mathrm{E}[\bar X]=p and \mathrm{Var}(\bar X)=v/n. Thus \mathrm{E}[\bar X^2]=\mathrm{Var}(\bar X)+\{\mathrm{E}[\bar X]\}^2 =\frac{v}{n}+p^2. Since T=\bar X-\bar X^2, \mathrm{E}[T]=p-p^2-\frac{v}{n}=\frac{n-1}{n}v, \qquad \mathrm{Bias}(T)=-\frac{v}{n}.

  2. Multiplying by c_n=n/(n-1) gives \mathrm{E}[c_nT]=v. This factor is finite because n\ge 2.

  3. Expanding the squared deviations and using X_i^2=X_i gives \sum_{i=1}^n(X_i-\bar X)^2 =\sum_{i=1}^n X_i^2-n\bar X^2 =n\bar X-n\bar X^2. Hence S^2=\frac{n}{n-1}\bar X(1-\bar X)=c_nT.

Important 101: Question 128: Comparing a Sample Mean with an Estimate that Uses a Benchmark

The mass of a filled container is modelled by independent observations X_i\sim\mathcal N(\mu,9), where the second parameter is the variance in squared grams. A technician uses a fixed target of 12 g when estimating the unknown mean mass \mu. The proposed estimator is T_n=\frac34\bar X+\frac14(12).

  1. Find the bias and variance of T_n.
  2. For a sample of size n=28, find the values of \mu for which T_n has strictly smaller MSE than \bar X.
  3. Consider the estimator U_n=(1-1/n)\bar X+12/n. Use its bias and variance to show that it is consistent for every fixed \mu.
Solution

  1. Since \mathrm{E}[\bar X]=\mu and \mathrm{Var}(\bar X)=9/n, \mathrm{Bias}(T_n)=\frac{12-\mu}{4}, \qquad \mathrm{Var}(T_n)=\left(\frac34\right)^2\frac{9}{n} =\frac{81}{16n}.

  2. The sample mean is unbiased, so \mathrm{MSE}(\bar X)=9/n. For T_n, \mathrm{MSE}(T_n)=\frac{81}{16n}+\frac{(12-\mu)^2}{16}. The strict inequality \mathrm{MSE}(T_n)<\mathrm{MSE}(\bar X) is equivalent to (12-\mu)^2<63/n. At n=28, this is |12-\mu|<1.5, or 10.5<\mu<13.5 g. The reduction in variance outweighs the squared bias within that range.

  3. The bias is (12-\mu)/n and the variance is (1-1/n)^2\,9/n. These both tend to zero for each fixed \mu. Hence \mathrm{MSE}(U_n)\to 0 and U_n is consistent.

Important 102: Question 129: Maximum Likelihood for a Gamma Rate

An instrument measures the elapsed time, in minutes, to complete four stages of a cycle. Model the elapsed times X_1,\ldots,X_n from independent cycles as \mathrm{Gamma}(4,\lambda), with known shape 4 and unknown rate \lambda>0. The density for one cycle is f(x\mid\lambda)=\frac{\lambda^4}{\Gamma(4)}x^3 e^{-\lambda x}, \qquad x>0.

  1. Write down the likelihood. Give the log-likelihood up to an additive constant independent of \lambda.
  2. Obtain the MLE of \lambda. Evaluate it for n=16 observed cycles with \sum_{i=1}^{16} x_i=80 minutes.
  3. Find the expected Fisher information for the whole sample, then evaluate it at the estimate from part 2.
Solution

  1. With S=\sum_{i=1}^n x_i, independence gives L(\lambda) =\frac{\prod_{i=1}^n x_i^3}{\Gamma(4)^n} \lambda^{4n}e^{-\lambda S}. Therefore \ell(\lambda)=4n\log\lambda-\lambda S+C.

  2. The score is \ell'(\lambda)=4n/\lambda-S. Setting it to zero gives \widehat\lambda=4n/S=4/\bar x. The second derivative is -4n/\lambda^2<0, so the stationary point maximises \ell. The data give \widehat\lambda=64/80=0.8 per minute.

  3. The second derivative is constant in the observations. Hence \mathcal I_n(\lambda) =-\mathrm{E}_\lambda[\ell''(\lambda)] =\frac{4n}{\lambda^2}. Evaluating at the estimate gives \mathcal I_{16}(0.8)=64/(0.8)^2=100.

Important 103: Question 130: Locating the Maximum Likelihood Estimate of a Weibull Shape

Three independent device lifetimes have a \mathrm{Weibull}(k,1) model, with unknown shape k>0 and scale fixed at 1. In the units used for the lifetimes, the density is f(x\mid k)=k x^{k-1}\exp(-x^k), \qquad x>0. The observed lifetimes are \tfrac12, 1 and 2. You may use \log 2\approx 0.6931.

  1. Derive the log-likelihood \ell(k) for the three observations. Simplify it using \log(\tfrac12)+\log 1+\log 2=0.
  2. Find the score \ell'(k) and evaluate it at k=1 and k=2.
  3. Show that \ell''(k)<0 for k>0. Use this fact and your score values to give a bracket for the unique MLE of k.
Solution

  1. Independence gives a product of three densities. Taking logs, \ell(k)=3\log k+(k-1)\bigl(\log(\tfrac12)+\log 1+\log 2\bigr) -\bigl((\tfrac12)^k+1+2^k\bigr) =3\log k-2^{-k}-1-2^k.

  2. Differentiating gives \ell'(k)=3/k-(\log 2)(2^k-2^{-k}). At the two trial values, \ell'(1)=3-1.5\log 2\approx 1.960, \qquad \ell'(2)=1.5-3.75\log 2\approx -1.099.

  3. A second differentiation gives \ell''(k)=-\frac{3}{k^2}-(\log 2)^2(2^k+2^{-k})<0. The score is continuous and strictly decreasing, and it changes sign between 1 and 2. It therefore has exactly one zero, with the log-likelihood increasing to that zero and decreasing after it. Thus 1<\hat k<2.

Important 104: Question 131: Comparing Likelihoods for Negative Binomial Counts

For each of three independent runs, a sensor sends packets until two packets have arrived intact. With a fixed probability p that a packet arrives intact and independent transmissions, let X_i be the number of packets sent in run i. Its Negative Binomial model has mass function \Pr(X_i=x\mid p)=(x-1)p^2(1-p)^{x-2}, \qquad x=2,3,\ldots. The only settings under consideration have p\in\{1/4,1/2,3/4\}. The observed counts are 3, 5 and 4.

  1. Write down and simplify the likelihood L(p) for the observed counts.
  2. Calculate the likelihood of each setting as a proportion of L(1/2). Give the maximum likelihood estimate within the specified parameter set.
  3. Explain how the counts of packets that did and did not arrive intact account for the two settings that give equal likelihood.
Solution

  1. Multiplying the three mass functions gives L(p)=(2\cdot4\cdot3)p^{2+2+2}(1-p)^{1+3+2} =24p^6(1-p)^6=24\{p(1-p)\}^6.

  2. At p=1/2, the relative likelihood is 1. At both p=1/4 and p=3/4, it is \frac{L(p)}{L(1/2)}=\left(\frac{3/16}{1/4}\right)^6 =\left(\frac34\right)^6\approx 0.178. The greatest likelihood in the given parameter set occurs at p=1/2, so \hat p=1/2.

  3. Each run contributes two intact packets, so six arrived intact overall. Of the 3+5+4=12 packets sent, six therefore failed to arrive intact. The likelihood contains equal powers of p and 1-p. Complementary settings 1/4 and 3/4 exchange those factors and fit these counts equally well.

Important 105: Question 132: A Large-Sample Interval for Mean Waiting Time

The time X_i from a passenger’s arrival at a stop to the next shuttle is modelled as Uniform on (0,\theta), in minutes, with unknown \theta>0. The n waiting times are independent and have mean \mu=\theta/2 and variance \theta^2/12. In a sample of n=100 waiting times the mean was \bar x=4.50 minutes. You may use z_{0.975}=1.960.

  1. State the large-sample approximate distribution of \bar X, with its mean and variance expressed in terms of \mu and n.
  2. Use \bar x as an estimate of \mu when computing the standard error. Find the resulting approximate two-sided 95\% confidence interval for \mu.
  3. Explain the meaning of the 95\% confidence level in repeated sampling and state why the coverage is approximate here.
Solution

  1. Because \theta=2\mu, the population variance is \mu^2/3. The central limit theorem gives \bar X\ \overset{\text{approx.}}{\sim}\ \mathcal N\left(\mu,\frac{\mu^2}{3n}\right).

  2. Substituting \bar x into the standard error gives \widehat{\mathrm{SE}}(\bar X)=\frac{4.50}{\sqrt{3\cdot100}}\approx 0.2598\ \text{minutes}. The interval is 4.50\pm 1.960(0.2598), approximately (3.991,\,5.009) minutes.

  3. Across repeated independent samples of size 100, approximately 95\% of intervals constructed by this method would contain the fixed \mu. The method relies on a large-sample Normal approximation for the Uniform sample mean and uses an estimated standard error.

Important 106: Question 133: A Normal Confidence Interval and Sample Size for a Target Width

A technician models the mass of independently sampled tablets as X_i\sim\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2), with unknown mean \mu and known standard deviation \sigma=0.9 mg. The mean of the first 16 observations is 11.4 mg. Use z_{0.95}=1.645.

  1. Compute an exact two-sided 90\% confidence interval for \mu using the first 16 observations.
  2. For a new sample from the same model, what is the smallest sample size that gives a two-sided 90\% confidence interval of total width at most 0.40 mg?
  3. Explain how changing the observed sample mean, while keeping the sample size, variance and confidence level fixed, affects the centre and width of the interval.
Solution

  1. The known standard deviation gives a standard error of 0.9/\sqrt{16}=0.225 mg. The exact 90\% interval is 11.4\pm 1.645(0.225), approximately (11.030,\,11.770) mg.

  2. The total width is 2(1.645)(0.9)/\sqrt n. The target requires n\ge \left(\frac{2(1.645)(0.9)}{0.40}\right)^2 \approx 54.80. The smallest integer sample size is 55.

  3. The centre is the observed sample mean, so it moves by the same amount as that mean. The width depends only on n, \sigma and the confidence level, so it stays fixed.

Important 107: Question 134: An Approximate MLE-Based Interval for a Normal Variance

A calibrated gauge has independent errors X_1,\ldots,X_n\sim\mathcal N(0,\theta), measured in millimetres. The mean 0 is known and the error variance \theta>0 is unknown. The recorded errors have n=200 and \sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2=450 squared millimetres. You may use the expected Fisher information \mathcal I_n(\theta)=n/(2\theta^2) and z_{0.975}=1.960.

  1. Derive the MLE of \theta and evaluate it for these data.
  2. Use the large-sample distribution of the MLE to estimate its standard error and compute an approximate two-sided 95\% confidence interval for \theta.
  3. A colleague suggests using the usual one-sample Student-t interval instead. Which parameter would that interval target, and why is it unsuitable for the objective here?
Solution

  1. The Normal model with known mean gives the log-likelihood \ell(\theta)=-\frac n2\log\theta-\frac{S}{2\theta}+C, \qquad S=\sum_{i=1}^n x_i^2. The score is (S-n\theta)/(2\theta^2). For these data, it is positive below S/n and negative above S/n, so the MLE is \widehat\theta=S/n=2.25 squared millimetres.

  2. The plug-in standard error is \sqrt{\mathcal I_n(\widehat\theta)^{-1}} =\widehat\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}} =2.25\sqrt{\frac{2}{200}}=0.225. Thus the approximate 95\% interval is 2.25\pm1.960(0.225)=(1.809,\,2.691) squared millimetres.

  3. The usual one-sample Student-t interval targets an unknown population mean, using the sample variance to estimate the standard error of that mean. Here the mean is already known and the target is the population variance \theta itself.

Important 108: Question 135: A Lower Endpoint for Recording Delays

A recorder has an unknown minimum startup delay \theta\geq 0 seconds. Independent startup times X_1,\ldots,X_n have density f(x\mid\theta)= \begin{cases} 2e^{-2(x-\theta)}, & x\geq\theta,\\ 0, & x<\theta. \end{cases} Use the stated value of the density at x=\theta.

  1. Write down the likelihood for a sample x_1,\ldots,x_n and give the range of \theta on which it is positive.

  2. Derive the maximum likelihood estimator of \theta. Explain how the likelihood behaves on its positive range.

  3. Find the estimate for the observed times 2.4, 2.1, 3.8, and 2.9 seconds. An analyst proposes using the sample mean instead. Explain what likelihood that proposal gives for these observations.

Solution

(a) Every observation must satisfy x_i\geq\theta. For a sample from this model, write x_{(1)}=\min_i x_i\geq 0. Independence gives L(\theta)= \begin{cases} 2^n\exp\!\left(-2\sum_{i=1}^n x_i+2n\theta\right), & 0\leq\theta\leq x_{(1)},\\ 0, & \theta>x_{(1)}. \end{cases}

(b) On [0,x_{(1)}], the factor \exp(2n\theta) is strictly increasing in \theta. The stipulated density includes the endpoint, so the likelihood attains its maximum at \hat\theta=\min_i X_i.

(c) The maximum likelihood estimate is 2.1 seconds. The sample mean is 2.8 seconds. Using \theta=2.8 would assign density zero to the observed times 2.1 and 2.4, so the joint likelihood at the sample mean is zero.

Important 109: Question 136: Credible Regions for Two Calibration Ranges

A calibration parameter \theta has posterior density 0.4 on each of the intervals [0,1] and [3,4], and density 0.1 on (1,3). The density is zero elsewhere. The following sets each contain 80\% posterior probability: C_1=[0.25,3.75], \qquad C_2=[0,1]\cup[3,4].

  1. Explain what the quoted 80\% probability means and which of C_1 and C_2 can be called a credible interval.

  2. Use the definition of a highest-density region to determine which of these two sets is an 80\% highest-density region. Give a density comparison that rules out the other set.

  3. A technician reads C_1 as saying that the central range (1,3) is where most of the posterior probability lies. Find the posterior probability of that range and explain how the two candidate sets can have the same probability despite covering different values.

Solution

(a) Conditional on the prior, model and data used to obtain the posterior, \Pr(\theta\in C_j\mid\underline{x})=0.8 for either set. Both sets are credible regions. C_1 is an interval because it is connected, while C_2 consists of two disjoint intervals.

(b) A highest-density region contains the points with the greatest posterior density up to the requested probability. For example, the density threshold 0.2 gives exactly C_2, which has probability 0.8. By contrast, C_1 includes \theta=2, where the density is 0.1, while excluding \theta=0.1, where it is 0.4. No density threshold can include the former without also including the latter.

(c) The central range has posterior probability (3-1)(0.1)=0.2. Relative to C_2, C_1 includes this central range and omits two outer pieces of length 0.25 with density 0.4. The pieces omitted have combined probability 2(0.25)(0.4)=0.2. The exchanged probability is the same.

Important 110: Question 137: A One-Sided Test for Mean Processing Time

A workshop wants to know whether a machine’s mean processing time \mu has increased from 20 minutes. Independent processing times X_i have a Gamma distribution with shape 4 and rate 4/\mu, so \mathrm{E}[X_i]=\mu and \mathrm{Var}(X_i)=\mu^2/4. The mean of n=64 observations is 22.5 minutes. Use a 5\% level and take the standard Normal CDF value \Phi(2)\approx 0.97725.

  1. State the hypotheses and use the Central Limit Theorem to give the approximate null distribution of the sample mean.

  2. Construct a standardised test statistic, compute the observed value and p-value, and state the decision in context.

  3. Explain what a Type I error would mean for the workshop and why the calculation in (b) uses the upper tail.

Solution

(a) The hypotheses are H_0:\mu=20 and H_1:\mu>20. The observations are i.i.d. with finite variance. Under H_0, the Central Limit Theorem therefore gives \bar X\ \overset{\mathrm{approx.}}{\sim}\ \mathcal N\!\left(20,\frac{20^2}{4\cdot 64}\right). The null standard error is 20/\sqrt{4\cdot 64}=1.25 minutes.

(b) The statistic Z=(\bar X-20)/1.25 is approximately standard Normal under H_0. Its observed value is (22.5-20)/1.25=2. The upper-tail p-value is about 1-0.97725=0.02275. Since 0.02275<0.05, reject H_0. The data provide evidence that the mean processing time has increased.

(c) A Type I error is concluding that the processing time has increased when the true mean is 20 minutes. The alternative concerns an increase, so large positive values of Z provide evidence in that direction.

Important 111: Question 138: Exact Size and Power for a Germination Test

A small greenhouse experiment uses five seeds. Each seed germinates independently with probability p, so the count X that germinates has a \mathrm{Binomial}(5,p) distribution. Test H_0:p=0.4 against H_1:p>0.4. The proposed tests reject when X\geq c for an integer cutoff c.

  1. Find the smallest cutoff giving a test of level 5\%. Calculate the achieved size of this test.

  2. Four of the five seeds germinate. Find the exact one-sided p-value and state what the study can conclude about p at the 5\% level.

  3. Find the power and the Type II error probability when p=0.8.

Solution

(a) The null upper-tail probabilities at the two largest attainable counts are \Pr_{0.4}(X\geq 4)=5(0.4)^4(0.6)+(0.4)^5=0.08704, \qquad \Pr_{0.4}(X\geq 5)=(0.4)^5=0.01024. Smaller cutoffs only increase the tail probability. Hence c=5 is the smallest cutoff with size no more than 0.05. Its achieved size is 0.01024 because H_0 specifies a single value of p.

(b) The p-value is \Pr_{0.4}(X\geq4)=0.08704. Fail to reject H_0 at the 5\% level. The five observations give insufficient evidence to conclude that the germination probability exceeds 0.4.

(c) At p=0.8, power is the probability of reaching the same rejection region, \Pr_{0.8}(X=5)=(0.8)^5=0.32768. The Type II error probability at this alternative is 1-0.32768=0.67232.

Important 112: Question 139: Updating the Rate at Which a Queue Clears

Four independent clearance times X_i, measured in minutes, have an Exponential distribution with rate \lambda>0. Their observed total is 12 minutes. The prior for \lambda is \mathrm{Gamma}(3,2) in shape–rate form. A \mathrm{Gamma}(a,b) density is proportional to \lambda^{a-1}e^{-b\lambda} and has mean a/b.

  1. Derive the posterior distribution of \lambda.

  2. Calculate the MAP estimate by maximising the log posterior.

  3. Calculate the posterior mean and express it as a weighted average of the prior mean and the maximum likelihood estimate from the clearance times. State what the weights are proportional to.

Solution

(a) The likelihood is proportional to \lambda^4e^{-12\lambda}. Multiplying by the prior kernel \lambda^2e^{-2\lambda} gives \lambda^6e^{-14\lambda}. Thus \lambda\mid\underline{x}\sim\mathrm{Gamma}(7,14).

(b) Up to a constant the log posterior is 6\log\lambda-14\lambda. Its derivative is 6/\lambda-14, which vanishes at \hat\lambda_{\mathrm{MAP}}=6/14=3/7 per minute. The second derivative -6/\lambda^2 is negative on the positive half-line, so this is the maximum.

(c) The log-likelihood has derivative 4/\lambda-12, so the MLE is 4/12=1/3 per minute. The prior mean is 3/2 per minute and the posterior mean is \mathrm{E}[\lambda\mid\underline{x}] =\frac{7}{14} =\frac{2}{14}\frac{3}{2}+\frac{12}{14}\frac{4}{12} =0.5 per minute. The weights are proportional to the prior rate hyperparameter 2 and the total observed clearance time 12.

Important 113: Question 140: A Posterior with a Bounded Prior

A sorting station diverts each inspected item to a manual check independently with probability p. A mechanical limit ensures 0\leq p\leq 1/2. The prior density is \pi(p)=8p on this closed interval and zero elsewhere. In a short trial, both of two inspected items are diverted. Use the stated closed support for the posterior density when evaluating its mode.

  1. Derive the normalised posterior density of p.

  2. Find the posterior mean and the MAP estimate.

  3. Find the prior and posterior probabilities of p>1/4. Interpret how observing the two diversions changes the probability of this event.

Solution

(a) The observed data have likelihood p^2. Multiplying by the prior gives 8p^3 on [0,1/2]. This kernel integrates to 8(1/2)^4/4=1/8, so the posterior density is 64p^3 on [0,1/2] and zero elsewhere.

(b) The posterior mean is \int_0^{1/2}p(64p^3)\,\mathrm{d}p =\frac{64}{5}(1/2)^5=\frac25. The posterior density increases up to the included upper endpoint. The MAP estimate is therefore 1/2.

(c) Before the trial the probability is \Pr(p>1/4)=\int_{1/4}^{1/2}8p\,\mathrm{d}p =4\bigl((1/2)^2-(1/4)^2\bigr)=\frac34. After the trial it is \Pr(p>1/4\mid\underline{x})=\int_{1/4}^{1/2}64p^3\,\mathrm{d}p =16\bigl((1/2)^4-(1/4)^4\bigr)=\frac{15}{16}. Larger values of p make two diversions more likely. The observations raise the probability that the station diverts more than a quarter of the items, from 0.75 to 0.9375.

Important 114: Question 141: Likelihood Ratio Evidence from Connection Attempts

A monitoring device records the number of attempts needed to connect after each restart. Independently for each restart, this count X_i has probability mass function f(x\mid p)=p(1-p)^{x-1},\qquad x=1,2,\ldots,\quad 0<p<1. The technical specification assumes a success probability of 1/2 on each attempt. Test H_0:p=1/2 against H_1:p\ne 1/2. Write S=\sum_{i=1}^n X_i, and assume S>n for the samples considered.

  1. Obtain the log-likelihood for p and derive its maximum likelihood estimator.

  2. Express the likelihood ratio test statistic in terms of n, S and the observed MLE. State its approximate null distribution for large n and the rejection rule at significance level \alpha.

  3. The device records a total of S=150 attempts across n=60 restarts. Compute the observed likelihood ratio statistic. Use the supplied quantiles to decide whether to reject at 5\% and at 1\%. Give bounds on the approximate p-value and interpret the result at 5\%.

  4. An independent run with n=30 and S=75 gives the same sample mean. Explain why the two runs have the same MLE but different test statistics. State whether the second run rejects at 5\% using the same approximation.

You may use \log(0.8)\approx -0.22314 and \log(1.2)\approx 0.18232. The 0.95 and 0.99 quantiles of \chi^2_1 are 3.841 and 6.635, respectively.

Solution

(a) Independence gives L(p)=p^n(1-p)^{S-n}. The log-likelihood and its first derivative are \ell(p)=n\log p +(S-n)\log(1-p),\qquad \ell'(p)=\frac{n}{p}-\frac{S-n}{1-p}. Setting the derivative to zero gives n(1-p)=(S-n)p, or \hat p=n/S=1/\bar X. This is an interior maximum since S>n and \ell is strictly concave.

(b) The likelihood ratio statistic is D=2\{\ell(\hat p)-\ell(1/2)\} =2\left[n\log(2\hat p)+(S-n)\log\{2(1-\hat p)\}\right]. Under the null, D is approximately \chi^2_1 for large n. Reject when D exceeds the (1-\alpha) quantile of \chi^2_1.

(c) Here \hat p=60/150=0.4 and D=2[60\log(0.8)+90\log(1.2)]\approx 6.04. Since 3.841<6.04<6.635, reject at 5\% and fail to reject at 1\%. The approximate p-value lies between 0.01 and 0.05. At the 5\% level there is evidence that the success probability differs from the specification of 1/2. The fitted value is lower than the specification.

(d) Both runs give \bar x=2.5 and \hat p=0.4. The formula in (b) has the same log terms for each run, but the coefficients n and S-n in the second run are half as large. This gives D\approx 3.02, below 3.841, so the second run fails to reject at 5\%. The likelihood accumulates evidence from each restart, so the same estimate gives stronger evidence in the larger sample.

Important 115: Question 142: Estimating an Upper Limit from a Uniform Sample

A dispenser releases a variable amount of liquid. Let X_1,\ldots,X_n be independent observations from \operatorname{Uniform}(0,\theta), where \theta>0 is the unknown upper limit in millilitres. Take the density to be 1/\theta on [0,\theta] and zero elsewhere. Let M_n=\max(X_1,\ldots,X_n) and assume n\ge 2.

  1. Write down the likelihood for an observed sample and find the maximum likelihood estimator of \theta, assuming the observed sample maximum is positive.
  2. Find the CDF of M_n and use its density to obtain \mathrm E[M_n] and \mathrm E[M_n^2].
  3. Find a constant c_n such that T_n=c_n M_n is unbiased for \theta. Calculate the mean squared error of T_n and use it to establish consistency.
  4. The alternative estimator is U_n=2\bar X. Show that it is unbiased, compare its mean squared error with that of T_n, and identify the smaller one. For a sample of 12 releases with maximum 49.5 mL and mean 27.3 mL, calculate T_n and U_n.
Solution

  1. Write m=\max_i x_i. The likelihood for positive observations is L(\theta;\underline x)=\theta^{-n}\mathbf 1_{\{\theta\ge m\}}. It decreases on the set of feasible values with positive likelihood. The largest likelihood is at \theta=m, so \hat\theta_{\mathrm{MLE}}=M_n.

  2. For 0\le t\le \theta, independence gives F_{M_n}(t)=\Pr(X_1\le t,\ldots,X_n\le t)=\left(\frac t\theta\right)^n. The CDF is zero for t<0 and one for t>\theta. Differentiating on (0,\theta) gives density nt^{n-1}/\theta^n. For j=1,2, \mathrm E[M_n^j] =\int_0^\theta t^j\frac{nt^{n-1}}{\theta^n}\,\mathrm dt =\frac{n}{n+j}\theta^j. Thus \mathrm E[M_n]=n\theta/(n+1) and \mathrm E[M_n^2]=n\theta^2/(n+2).

  3. Take c_n=(n+1)/n. The resulting estimator is unbiased and \begin{aligned} \operatorname{MSE}(T_n) &=\operatorname{Var}(T_n) =\left(\frac{n+1}{n}\right)^2\frac{n\theta^2}{n+2}-\theta^2\\ &=\frac{\theta^2}{n(n+2)}. \end{aligned} For any \varepsilon>0, \Pr(|T_n-\theta|\ge\varepsilon)\le \operatorname{MSE}(T_n)/\varepsilon^2\to 0. Hence T_n is consistent.

  4. Since \mathrm E[X_i]=\theta/2 and \operatorname{Var}(X_i)=\theta^2/12, we have \mathrm E[U_n]=\theta and \operatorname{MSE}(U_n)=\operatorname{Var}(U_n)=\frac{4}{n}\frac{\theta^2}{12}=\frac{\theta^2}{3n}. Since n+2>3 for n\ge2, T_n has the smaller MSE for every \theta>0. The observed estimates are T_{12}=\frac{13}{12}(49.5)=53.625\text{ mL},\qquad U_{12}=2(27.3)=54.6\text{ mL}.

Important 116: Question 143: Comparing Rate Estimators with Unequal Observation Times

A fault monitor records counts of brief interruptions in n disjoint observation windows. Window i lasts a known t_i>0 hours. The counts X_i are independent with X_i\sim \operatorname{Poisson}(\lambda t_i), where \lambda\ge0 is the common hourly rate. Define S=\sum_{i=1}^n X_i,\qquad T=\sum_{i=1}^n t_i,\qquad A=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{X_i}{t_i}.

  1. Write down the likelihood for \lambda and find its maximum likelihood estimator. Include the case S=0.
  2. Show whether the MLE and A are unbiased for \lambda. Derive their variances.
  3. Obtain the expected Fisher information for \lambda>0 in the full sample. Identify which estimator attains the Cramér–Rao lower bound. State when A has the same variance as the MLE. You may use \left(\sum_i t_i\right)\left(\sum_i 1/t_i\right)\ge n^2, with equality exactly when the t_i are all equal.
  4. For four windows with lengths (2,4,6,8) and counts (3,11,16,22), calculate both rate estimates and the ratio \operatorname{Var}(A)/\operatorname{Var}(\hat\lambda_{\mathrm{MLE}}) for \lambda>0. Explain why the estimators weight the observed rates differently.
Solution

  1. With s=\sum_i x_i, independence gives L(\lambda;\underline x) =\left(\prod_{i=1}^n\frac{t_i^{x_i}}{x_i!}\right)\lambda^s e^{-\lambda T}. For s>0 and \lambda>0, \ell'(\lambda)=s/\lambda-T is zero at \lambda=s/T and changes from positive to negative there. For s=0, e^{-\lambda T} decreases on [0,\infty) and is maximised at zero. Thus \hat\lambda_{\mathrm{MLE}}=S/T in either case.

  2. The Poisson means and variances are \lambda t_i. Hence \mathrm E[S/T]=\lambda,\qquad \operatorname{Var}(S/T)=\lambda/T. Also \mathrm E[X_i/t_i]=\lambda and \operatorname{Var}(X_i/t_i)=\lambda/t_i. Therefore \mathrm E[A]=\lambda,\qquad \operatorname{Var}(A)=\frac{\lambda}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{t_i}.

  3. The second derivative is \ell''(\lambda)=-S/\lambda^2. Its negative expectation is \mathcal I_n(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda T}{\lambda^2}=\frac T\lambda. The MLE is unbiased and has variance \mathcal I_n(\lambda)^{-1}, so it attains the bound for \lambda>0. The given inequality yields \operatorname{Var}(A)\ge\lambda/T. For \lambda>0 equality holds when all window lengths are equal.

  4. The total count is 52 over 20 hours, giving S/T=2.60 per hour. The unweighted average rate is A=\tfrac14\left(\tfrac32+\tfrac{11}4+\tfrac{16}6+\tfrac{22}8\right) =\tfrac{29}{12}\approx 2.42\text{ per hour}. The variance ratio at any \lambda>0 is \frac{T}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{t_i} =\frac{20}{16}\left(\frac12+\frac14+\frac16+\frac18\right) =\frac{125}{96}\approx1.30. The MLE can be written as \sum_i(t_i/T)(X_i/t_i). It gives more weight to longer windows because their individual rate estimates have smaller variances. A weights the windows equally.

Important 117: Question 144: Bias Correction for a Lognormal Mean

The time X_i taken for a coating to dry is positive. The observations are independent and Y_i=\log X_i\sim \mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2), where \mu\in\mathbb R is unknown and \sigma^2>0 is known. The arithmetic mean drying time is m=\mathrm E[X_i]=e^{\mu+\sigma^2/2}. You may use \mathrm E[e^{tZ}]=e^{ta+t^2v/2} for Z\sim\mathcal N(a,v).

  1. Write down the log-likelihood for \mu in terms of y_i=\log x_i, up to a term independent of \mu, and derive the MLE of \mu.
  2. Find the MLE \hat m of the arithmetic mean and show that it is biased at finite n. State the direction of bias.
  3. Find a multiplicative correction to \hat m that makes the corrected estimator \tilde m unbiased. Derive \operatorname{Var}(\tilde m) and use it to show that \tilde m is consistent.
  4. A sample of 25 observations has \bar y=\log 20 and \sigma^2=0.36. Compute the two estimates of m. With the same model, what factor would multiply the variance of \tilde m if the sample size increased from 25 to 50?
Solution

  1. The log-likelihood, up to a term independent of \mu, is \ell(\mu)=-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i-\mu)^2. Using the Y_i instead of the X_i discards only a Jacobian factor independent of \mu. The score is n(\bar y-\mu)/\sigma^2, so \hat\mu=\bar Y.

  2. Invariance of maximum likelihood estimates under this one-to-one transformation gives \hat m=e^{\bar Y+\sigma^2/2}. Since \bar Y\sim\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2/n), \mathrm E[\hat m]=e^{\sigma^2/2}e^{\mu+\sigma^2/(2n)} =m e^{\sigma^2/(2n)}>m. The bias is positive.

  3. Set \tilde m=e^{-\sigma^2/(2n)}\hat m, so \mathrm E[\tilde m]=m. Applying the given formula with t=2 gives \mathrm E[\tilde m^2] =e^{\sigma^2-\sigma^2/n}e^{2\mu+2\sigma^2/n} =m^2 e^{\sigma^2/n}. Thus \operatorname{Var}(\tilde m)=m^2(e^{\sigma^2/n}-1). It tends to zero as n increases. Since \tilde m is unbiased, its MSE tends to zero, which proves consistency.

  4. The MLE is 20e^{0.18}\approx23.94 and the corrected estimate is 20e^{0.18-0.36/50}\approx23.77, in the original time units. The variance at n=50 is the variance at n=25 multiplied by \frac{e^{0.36/50}-1}{e^{0.36/25}-1}\approx0.498.

Important 118: Question 145: Likelihood from Two Lifetime Thresholds

A device’s lifetime has an Exponential distribution with rate \lambda>0 per hour. Its exact lifetime is unavailable, but a status check records whether the device is still working at a specified time. Set p=e^{-\lambda}. The first group of n devices is checked after one hour and the second group of m different devices is checked after two hours. The binary readings are all independent. The first group has readings X_i\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p) and the second has readings Z_j\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p^2). Let x and z be the observed totals of the two groups.

  1. Obtain the likelihood and log-likelihood of p in terms of x,z,n,m.
  2. Show that an interior MLE satisfies (n+2m)p^2+(n-x)p-(x+2z)=0. For n=60, m=48, x=36, and z=18, find the MLEs of p and \lambda.
  3. Obtain the expected Fisher information for p from the full set of readings.
  4. Using the large-sample approximation \hat p\overset{\mathrm{approx.}}{\sim}\mathcal N(p,\mathcal I(p)^{-1}) for this two-group model, calculate an approximate 95\% confidence interval for p with these data. Use z_{0.975}=1.960.
Solution

  1. Multiplying the Bernoulli likelihood factors gives L(p)=p^x(1-p)^{n-x}(p^2)^z(1-p^2)^{m-z}. The log-likelihood for the individual binary readings is \ell(p)=(x+2z)\log p+(n-x)\log(1-p)+(m-z)\log(1-p^2).

  2. Differentiating gives \ell'(p)=\frac{x+2z}{p}-\frac{n-x}{1-p}-\frac{2p(m-z)}{1-p^2}. Multiply \ell'(p)=0 by p(1-p^2) and collect terms. This gives the stated equation. With the observed data it becomes 156p^2+24p-72=0, whose only root in (0,1) is \hat p=\frac{-24+\sqrt{24^2+4(156)(72)}}{312}\approx0.6068. The likelihood tends to zero at both endpoints for these data, so this is the maximum. By invariance, \hat\lambda=-\log\hat p\approx0.4996 per hour.

  3. Differentiating again gives -\ell''(p)=\frac{x+2z}{p^2}+\frac{n-x}{(1-p)^2}+\frac{2(m-z)(1+p^2)}{(1-p^2)^2}. Use \mathrm E[x]=np and \mathrm E[z]=mp^2, with the totals viewed as random in this expectation. The first group gives n/p+n/(1-p) and the second gives 2m+2m(1+p^2)/(1-p^2). Thus \mathcal I(p)=\frac{n}{p(1-p)}+\frac{4m}{1-p^2}.

  4. Evaluating the information at \hat p gives \mathcal I(\hat p)\approx555.36 and estimated standard error 1/\sqrt{\mathcal I(\hat p)}\approx0.04243. The interval is \hat p\pm1.960(0.04243)\approx(0.524,\ 0.690).

Important 119: Question 146: Bayesian Precision with Unequal Measurement Weights

A technician measures reference objects with known true values \mu_1,\ldots,\mu_n. The readings Y_i are conditionally independent with Y_i\mid\tau\sim\mathcal N\!\left(\mu_i,\frac{1}{w_i\tau}\right), where w_i>0 are known measurement weights and \tau>0 is the unknown baseline precision. Write S=\sum_i w_i(y_i-\mu_i)^2 for the observed weighted sum of squared errors. Take the prior to be \tau\sim\operatorname{Gamma}(4,3), using shape and rate.

  1. Obtain the likelihood kernel for \tau and use it to identify the posterior distribution in terms of n and S.
  2. In a set of 6 measurements with S=18, calculate the posterior mean and MAP estimate of \tau.
  3. Let v=1/\tau be the baseline variance. Identify its posterior distribution and calculate its posterior mean and MAP estimate. Compare the MAP of v with the reciprocal of the MAP of \tau. You may use that the reciprocal of a \operatorname{Gamma}(a,b) variable is \operatorname{InvGamma}(a,b), whose mean for a>1 is b/(a-1) and whose mode is b/(a+1).
  4. Calculate and interpret \Pr(v>2\mid\underline y) for these data. For a \operatorname{Gamma}(r,b) distribution with a positive integer shape r and rate b, you may use F(t)=1-e^{-bt}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\frac{(bt)^j}{j!},\qquad t\ge0.
Solution

  1. The density of each reading contributes a factor proportional to \tau^{1/2}\exp\{-w_i\tau (y_i-\mu_i)^2/2\}. Multiplication gives the likelihood kernel \tau^{n/2}\exp(-\tau S/2). Combining with the prior yields \pi(\tau\mid\underline y)\propto \tau^{4+n/2-1}e^{-(3+S/2)\tau},\qquad \tau>0, so \tau\mid\underline y\sim\operatorname{Gamma}(4+n/2,3+S/2).

  2. The posterior is \operatorname{Gamma}(7,12). Its mean is 7/12\approx0.5833. The derivative of the log posterior is 6/\tau-12, which changes from positive to negative at 6/12. The MAP estimate is 0.5.

  3. The reciprocal relationship gives v\mid\underline y\sim\operatorname{InvGamma}(7,12). Its posterior mean is 12/(7-1)=2 and its MAP estimate is 12/(7+1)=1.5. The reciprocal of the MAP for \tau is 1/0.5=2. Modes of continuous densities can change under reparameterisation because the transformed density includes a Jacobian factor. Here the factor is v^{-2}.

  4. Since \tau is positive, v>2 is equivalent to \tau<0.5. By continuity and the supplied CDF, \Pr(v>2\mid\underline y)=F(0.5)=1-e^{-6}\sum_{j=0}^{6}\frac{6^j}{j!}\approx0.3937. Given the data, model and prior, the baseline variance has about a 39.4\% probability of exceeding 2.

Important 120: Question 147: A Discrete Posterior for a Gamma Shape Parameter

A manufacturer models the elapsed time X for a sensor to stabilise as \operatorname{Gamma}(K,2), with shape K and rate 2. Previous tests suggest three possible values for K. The prior probabilities are \Pr(K=1)=0.7,\qquad \Pr(K=2)=0.1,\qquad \Pr(K=4)=0.2. Conditional on K, three independent times are observed as (0.5,1,1.5). You may use the density f(x\mid k)=2^k x^{k-1}e^{-2x}/\Gamma(k) for x>0 and \Gamma(1)=\Gamma(2)=1, \Gamma(4)=6.

  1. Find the likelihood at each of the three possible shapes relative to its value at K=1.
  2. Obtain the posterior mass function of K.
  3. Find its posterior mean, median and MAP estimate.
  4. Find a posterior credible set with probability at least 85\% that contains as few possible values of K as possible. Give its posterior probability and translate the set into possible values of the population mean stabilisation time.
Solution

  1. For a proposed shape k the likelihood is L(k)=\frac{2^{3k}}{\Gamma(k)^3}\left(\prod_{i=1}^3 x_i\right)^{k-1} e^{-2\sum_{i=1}^3x_i}. Here \prod_i x_i=3/4. Dividing by L(1) cancels the final exponential. The three relative likelihoods are 1,\qquad 2^3\left(\frac34\right)=6,\qquad \left(\frac{2^3}{6}\right)^3\left(\frac34\right)^3=1.

  2. Multiplying these values by the prior masses gives unnormalised weights (0.7,0.6,0.2), whose sum is 1.5. Therefore \bigl(\Pr(K=1\mid\underline x),\Pr(K=2\mid\underline x),\Pr(K=4\mid\underline x)\bigr) =\left(\frac7{15},\frac6{15},\frac2{15}\right).

  3. The posterior mean is (1\cdot7+2\cdot6+4\cdot2)/15=27/15=1.8. The cumulative probability is 7/15<1/2 at K=1 and 13/15>1/2 at K=2, so the median is 2. The largest posterior mass is at K=1, which is the MAP estimate.

  4. The largest single mass is 7/15, below 0.85. The set \{1,2\} contains two possibilities and has posterior mass 13/15\approx0.867, meeting the target. The population mean is K/2, so the data and prior give the mean a posterior probability of 13/15 of belonging to \{0.5,1\} in the original time units.

Important 121: Question 148: Posterior Uncertainty about a Damper’s Maximum Travel

Under randomly chosen loads, an adjustable damper travels distances X_1,\ldots,X_n that are independent \operatorname{Uniform}(0,\theta) observations conditional on the upper limit \theta. The density is 1/\theta on [0,\theta] and zero elsewhere. Analysis of the design gives a prior density \pi(\theta)=\alpha L_0^\alpha\theta^{-(\alpha+1)}\quad\text{for }\theta\ge L_0, and zero below L_0, where L_0>0 and \alpha>0 are known.

  1. Write down the likelihood and find the posterior kernel, including its support, in terms of L_0, \alpha, n, and the sample maximum m.
  2. Normalise the posterior density and obtain its CDF.
  3. Suppose L_0=40 mm, \alpha=2, n=4, and m=60 mm. Find the posterior median and the 90\% equal-tailed credible interval for \theta.
  4. Given the same data, calculate the posterior probability that the maximum travel exceeds 80 mm. Interpret this and the credible interval from part 3 in context.
Solution

  1. The likelihood is \theta^{-n} when \theta\ge m and is zero otherwise. Combining likelihood and prior gives a posterior kernel proportional to \theta^{-(\alpha+n+1)} for \theta\ge L=\max(L_0,m).

  2. Let a=\alpha+n. Since \int_L^\infty\theta^{-a-1}\,\mathrm d\theta=L^{-a}/a, the normalised density is a L^a\theta^{-a-1} on [L,\infty). The CDF for t\ge L is F(t)=\int_L^t a L^a\theta^{-a-1}\,\mathrm d\theta =1-(L/t)^a. It is zero below L.

  3. Here L=60 and a=6. Solving F(t)=u gives t=60(1-u)^{-1/6}. The median is 60(0.5)^{-1/6}\approx67.35 mm. The 5\% and 95\% posterior quantiles give the interval \bigl[60(0.95)^{-1/6},\ 60(0.05)^{-1/6}\bigr]\approx[60.52,\ 98.85]\text{ mm}.

  4. The posterior probability that the upper limit exceeds 80 mm is 1-F(80)=\left(\frac{60}{80}\right)^6\approx0.1780. Given the data, model and prior, the damper’s upper limit has about a 17.8\% chance of exceeding 80 mm and a 90\% chance of being within the interval in part 3. The equal-tailed construction assigns 5\% posterior probability to each side outside that interval.

Important 122: Question 149: A Shrinking MAP Estimate for a Normal Mean

A quality team measures n signed deviations from a target thickness. Conditional on the unknown mean deviation \mu, the measurements X_i are independent with X_i\sim\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2), where \sigma^2 is known. The prior density is \pi(\mu)=\frac{1}{2b}e^{-|\mu|/b},\qquad -\infty<\mu<\infty, with known b>0.

  1. Obtain the likelihood kernel as a function of \mu. Find the MLE and expected Fisher information for the full sample.
  2. Write down the posterior log-density up to an additive constant. By treating positive and negative \mu separately and considering \mu=0, derive a rule for the MAP estimate in terms of \bar x, \sigma^2, n, and b.
  3. For \sigma=4, b=1, and n=16, calculate the MAP estimate for an observed mean \bar x=1.25. Calculate it again for a hypothetical repeat with \bar x=-0.75. Explain how the prior influences these two estimates.
  4. A larger sample of 400 deviations with the same \sigma and b has \bar x=1.25. Give the large-sample Normal approximation to the posterior and use it to estimate \Pr(\mu>1\mid\underline x). State how the centring of this posterior approximation differs from the sampling distribution of the MLE. You may use \Phi(1.25)=0.8944.
Solution

  1. The identity \sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2+n(\bar x-\mu)^2 gives a likelihood kernel \exp\{-n(\bar x-\mu)^2/(2\sigma^2)\}. The MLE is \hat\mu=\bar X. The second derivative of the log-likelihood is -n/\sigma^2, so the information is \mathcal I_n(\mu)=n/\sigma^2.

  2. The posterior log-density, up to an additive constant, is -\frac{n}{2\sigma^2}(\mu-\bar x)^2-\frac{|\mu|}{b}. Put c=\sigma^2/(nb). For \mu>0 the derivative is n(\bar x-\mu)/\sigma^2-1/b, which vanishes at \bar x-c if \bar x>c. For \mu<0 it is n(\bar x-\mu)/\sigma^2+1/b, which vanishes at \bar x+c if \bar x<-c. The derivative decreases on each side and jumps down at zero. For -c\le\bar x\le c, the log-density increases up to zero and decreases thereafter.
