The resulting MAP rule is \hat\mu_{\mathrm{MAP}}= \begin{cases} \bar x-c,&\bar x>c,\\ 0,&|\bar x|\le c,\\ \bar x+c,&\bar x<-c. \end{cases}

  3. The threshold is c=4^2/(16\cdot1)=1. For \bar x=1.25 the MAP is 0.25. For \bar x=-0.75 it is zero. The prior has its peak at zero, so it shifts the mode towards zero. When the magnitude of \bar x lies within the threshold, the posterior mode sits at zero.

  4. The observed MLE is 1.25 and the inverse information is 16/400=0.04. Thus \mu\mid\underline x is approximately \mathcal N(1.25,0.04). Its standard deviation is 0.2 and the estimated posterior probability is 1-\Phi\!\left(\frac{1-1.25}{0.2}\right)=\Phi(1.25)=0.8944. The approximate posterior is centred at the MLE for the observed data. The sampling distribution of \hat\mu=\bar X is centred at the true \mu and describes how the estimate varies over repeated samples.

Important 123: Question 150: Likelihood ratio testing for instrument variance

A laboratory checks a balance by weighing the same reference mass repeatedly. Let X_i be the difference in milligrams between the ith reading and the reference value. The laboratory models X_1,\ldots,X_n as independent \mathcal N(0,v) observations with unknown variance v>0. It wishes to test H_0:v=1 against H_1:v\ne 1.

  1. Write down the log-likelihood for v and obtain its maximum likelihood estimator for samples with \sum_i x_i^2>0.
  2. Derive the likelihood ratio test statistic in terms of n and the observed maximum likelihood estimate. State its approximate distribution under H_0.
  3. For n=30, the laboratory obtains \sum_i x_i^2=51. Calculate the test statistic, bound the p-value using the reference values below, and decide whether to reject at the 5\% level.
  4. Find the expected Fisher information for the sample and use it to construct an approximate 95\% Normal confidence interval for v from these data. Does the test based on this interval give the same decision as the likelihood ratio test? Explain how the two decisions can differ.

You may use \log(1.7)=0.5306, z_{0.975}=1.960, and the 95th, 97.5th and 99th percentiles of \chi^2_1, which are 3.841, 5.024 and 6.635.

Solution

  1. With S=\sum_i x_i^2, independence gives the log-likelihood \ell(v)=-\frac n2\log(2\pi)-\frac n2\log v-\frac{S}{2v}. Its derivative is \ell'(v)=-\frac{n}{2v}+\frac{S}{2v^2}=\frac{S-nv}{2v^2}. For S>0, this changes sign from positive to negative at \hat v=S/n, so \hat v is the maximiser.

  2. The log-likelihood at \hat v has S/\hat v=n. At the null value v_0=1 it has S/v_0=n\hat v. Therefore D=2\{\ell(\hat v)-\ell(1)\} =n\{\hat v-1-\log\hat v\}. The full model has one free parameter and the null fixes it at an interior value. Under H_0, D is approximately \chi^2_1 for large n.

  3. Here \hat v=51/30=1.7 and D=30(1.7-1-0.5306)\approx 5.08. Since 5.024<D<6.635, the approximate upper-tail probability lies between 0.01 and 0.025. The result falls in the 5\% rejection region. There is evidence that the variance differs from 1 square milligram.

  4. Differentiate again and use \mathrm E_v[S]=nv: \ell''(v)=\frac{n}{2v^2}-\frac{S}{v^3}, \qquad \mathcal I_n(v)=-\mathrm E_v[\ell''(v)]=\frac{n}{2v^2}. The large-sample variance of the MLE is approximately 2v^2/n, giving the estimated standard error \hat v\sqrt{2/n}. The approximate interval is 1.7\pm 1.960(1.7)\sqrt{\frac{2}{30}}\approx (0.840,\ 2.560). It contains 1, so the corresponding Wald test fails to reject at 5\%. The interval uses a Normal approximation centred at \hat v with a plug-in standard error. The likelihood ratio test uses the difference in log-likelihoods and a \chi^2_1 reference distribution. The two approximate rules have different rejection regions at this sample size.

Important 124: Question 151: An exact test for a lognormal mean

An engineering team monitors the total energy Y_i in kilowatt-hours used by the ith run of a cleaning process. Assume the Y_i are independent lognormal observations such that W_i=\log Y_i\sim\mathcal N(\mu,0.16), where \log is the natural logarithm. The arithmetic mean energy is m=\mathrm E[Y_i]=\exp(\mu+0.08). The team wants to test H_0:m=8 against H_1:m>8.

  1. Express both hypotheses in terms of \mu and state the sampling distribution of \bar W.
  2. Derive a test of exact size \alpha based on \bar W. Give the rejection region in terms of \bar W and explain what a Type I error would mean here.
  3. A sample of 25 runs gives \overline{\log y}=2.16. Compute the standardised test statistic, bound the one-sided p-value using the reference values below, and report the conclusion at the 5\% level.
  4. Express the power function in terms of m and n. For the test at the 5\% level with n=25, evaluate the power when the true mean energy is 10 kilowatt-hours and interpret this probability.

You may use \log 8=2.07944, \log 10=2.30259, z_{0.95}=1.645, z_{0.975}=1.960, z_{0.99}=2.326, and \Phi(1.144)\approx0.874.

Solution

  1. The map \mu\mapsto\exp(\mu+0.08) is increasing. With \mu_0=\log 8-0.08, the hypotheses become H_0:\mu=\mu_0 and H_1:\mu>\mu_0. Independence and normality give \bar W\sim\mathcal N(\mu,0.16/n) exactly.

  2. Under H_0, Z=\frac{\bar W-\mu_0}{0.4/\sqrt n}\sim\mathcal N(0,1). Reject for Z>z_{1-\alpha}, or equivalently for \bar W>\mu_0+\frac{0.4}{\sqrt n}z_{1-\alpha}. This region has probability \alpha under H_0. A Type I error means concluding that the mean energy exceeds 8 kilowatt-hours when the mean is 8.

  3. The standard error is 0.4/5=0.08 and \mu_0\approx 1.99944, so z_{\rm obs}=\frac{2.16-1.99944}{0.08}\approx 2.007. Since z_{0.975}<z_{\rm obs}<z_{0.99}, the upper-tail p-value is between 0.01 and 0.025. The team rejects H_0 at 5\%. There is evidence that the arithmetic mean energy exceeds 8 kilowatt-hours.

  4. Under any m>0, \mu-\mu_0=\log(m/8), giving \begin{aligned} \mathrm{Power}(m) &=1-\Phi\!\left(z_{1-\alpha}-\frac{\sqrt n}{0.4}\log(m/8)\right)\\ &=\Phi\!\left(\frac{\sqrt n}{0.4}\log(m/8)-z_{1-\alpha}\right). \end{aligned} For m=10, \sqrt n/0.4=12.5, and the argument of \Phi is 12.5(2.30259-2.07944)-1.645\approx 1.144. The power is about 0.874. If the mean is 10 kilowatt-hours and the model holds, about 87.4\% of repeated studies with 25 runs would reject H_0 using this rule.

Important 125: Question 152: Testing dispatch times and planning for power

A warehouse wants to know whether a change has reduced the mean time to prepare an order. It models the preparation times X_1,\ldots,X_n in minutes as independent \mathrm{Uniform}(\mu-6,\mu+6) observations, with \mu>6. The previous mean was 24 minutes. The hypotheses are H_0:\mu=24 and H_1:\mu<24.

  1. State \mathrm E[X_i] and \mathrm{Var}(X_i) under the model. Give the approximate sampling distribution of \bar X for large n and name the result that justifies it.
  2. Give the critical value for a 5\% test in terms of \bar X. For a sample of 36 orders with \bar x=22.95, decide whether to reject and give a conclusion in context.
  3. Derive the approximate power function of this rule. At n=36 and \mu=22.5, find the power and the Type II error probability.
  4. The warehouse plans to repeat the study. Find the smallest integer sample size that gives at least 90\% power at \mu=22.8 under the Normal approximation, using the same 5\% level.

You may use z_{0.95}=1.645, z_{0.90}=1.282, and \Phi(0.953)\approx0.830.

Solution

  1. The midpoint of the Uniform interval is \mu and its width is 12. Thus \mathrm E[X_i]=\mu and \mathrm{Var}(X_i)=12^2/12=12. By the central limit theorem, \bar X \mathrel{\overset{\rm approx}{\sim}}\mathcal N(\mu,12/n) for large n.

  2. Under H_0, the lower 5\% critical value for \bar X is c_n=24-1.645\sqrt{12/n}. Reject if \bar X<c_n. At n=36, c_{36}\approx 23.050. The observed mean 22.95 is below this threshold, so the warehouse rejects H_0. The data provide evidence of a reduction in mean preparation time.

  3. For any \mu in the model, \Pr_\mu(\bar X<c_n) \approx \Phi\!\left(\frac{24-\mu}{\sqrt{12/n}}-1.645\right). At \mu=22.5, n=36, the argument is approximately 0.953. The power is 0.830 and the Type II error probability at this alternative is approximately 0.170.

  4. Power of at least 0.90 at \mu=22.8 requires 1.2\sqrt{n/12}-1.645\ \ge\ 1.282. Rearranging gives n\ge 12\left(\frac{1.645+1.282}{1.2}\right)^2\approx 71.39. The smallest integer that meets the requirement under the approximation is 72.
